1.	INTRODUCTION

Many IPM Projects around the world have developed their own operational checklists.  In other projects there are no checklists or they haven't yet been fully developed.  In these circumstances the Projects must rely on the knowledge, experience and common sense of their own supervisors to check over the operations at field level.

Different methods have been developed to set basic guidelines for operations, these include collecting the knowledge and lessons learned from various projects, as well as establishing its own Policies, Standards and Procedures.  The aim has been to assist project development, and to make full use of the knowledge and experience gained along the years.

The Rig Operations Performance Execution, ROPE, Manual is intended to establish a core set of guidelines and checklists for the different operations to be carried out on a rig.  The intention is that these checklists should be adapted and customized according to the projects own needs.

The ROPE Manual is based on the Policies and Standards, approved manuals (i.e. Well Control Manual, Stuck Pipe handbook, etc.), and oil field best practices.  It includes detailed step-by-step procedures for different rig operations, and is a compilation of the most complete set of checklists used in the company so far.

The ROPE Manual will be an active document that depends on feedback obtained from different Projects.  As it is implemented, it will be enriched and improved by this feedback and by the Quality Management System (Q.M.S.) philosophy.  All suggestions for improvements should be submitted as "Best Practices" or "Lessons Learnt" to InTouch.

2.	RIG OPERATIONS CHECKLISTS

2.1	BHA CHECKLIST

2.1.1	BHA COMPONENTS & MAKE UP  
•	Ensure that the BHA equipment is on board, or ordered in time, for next hole section as per requirements of the drilling programme or operational requirements.
•	Verify that all drill pipe, drill collars, crossovers, jars, etc, have been recently inspected and are in useable condition.  Use only two classes of drillpipe: (1) new (white band) or (2) premium used (two white bands)
•	Verify the condition of the drill pipe & HWDP, as follows:
1.	Caliper OD's, ID's & lengths of several tool joints to record for future capacity calculations.
2.	Check ID for obstructions.  Rabbit if possible
3.	Condition of plastic coated ID.
4.	Condition of tube body.
5.	Condition of tool joints.
6.	HWDP has API bore back stress relief groove on box end and API stress relief groove on pin end.
•	Verify the condition of drill collars, as follows:
1.	What are the actual OD's & ID’s caliper all.
2.	How many are greater than 30 ft and less than 28 ft?
3.	Do the inspection reports match the OD's, lengths, and serial #'s on the rig?
4.	Does pin end have API stress relief groove?
5.	Does box end have API bore back stress relief groove
6.	Are straight crossover subs a minimum of 36” overall?
7.	Are tapered crossover subs a minimum of 48” overall with a minimum fishing neck of 32”?
8.	Is bit sub recessed for a Baker float?
9.	Do the subs have the correct bevel for the surfaces that they are mating with?
•	Review handling practices with rig crew, to discuss rotary bushings, slips, tongs, slip setting practices, rig alignment, connection practices, pick up and storage practices, and margin of overpull, based upon API pipe classification.
•	Ensure that the slips are not damaging the drill pipe by crushing
•	Ensure that the slips designed for the particular size pipe being used.
•	Ensure that the gripping elements in the slips are not worn.
•	Keep the tool joint as low to the rotary table as practical during make-up and breakout.  (Refer to API RP 7G (DrillStem Design and Operating Limits)
•	Ensure that the tool joints are handled properly.
•	Ensure that the tool joints are torqued properly:
1.	API RP 7G shows the recommended make-up torques for commonly used tool-joints in Premium Class. For OD's between those shown, estimate the make-up torque (torque proportional to diameter) or use 60% of torsional yield.
Note: Most Drilling Contractors will have their own specifications for pipe make-up.  Review this information in case the string starts showing signs of under / over torquing.
2.	Make up new tool-joints very slowly and torque to minimum specified torque.  Then break out to inspect for minor damage.  Re-lubricate and make up to same torque.
3.	Ensure that the torque sensor is pumped up and the torque gauge is calibrated.
•	Pipe recovered in a fishing operation should be replaced or, as a minimum, inspected before it is run in the hole again.
•	Review the API RP 7G guidelines on Bending Strength Ratios (BSR) for recommendations in compatibility of DC's and connection combinations.
•	Verify that the Drilling Contractor's pipe handling practices are compatible and are good operating practices.
•	Ensure that all BHA's are calipered for OD & ID of every component, and a schematic is prepared, including lengths, prior to running in the hole.
•	Ensure that the ratings of the rig tongs are adequate for the torque applied.
•	Ensure an adequate supply of the correct type doping compound is available.
•	Ensure that any storage type of grease / dope is completely removed and proper dope is applied before make-up.

2.1.2	BHA OPERATION
•	Ensure that anticipated drilling objectives of next bit run are reviewed prior to make up of BHA.
•	Determine the optimum location for the drilling jars. Jars should normally be run in tension, however they can be run in compression in certain instances. Never run jars in neutral.
•	Ensure that the OD of the BHA components is not too large to be fished with overshots.
•	Verify weights available for drilling operations, ensuring the drill string is not in compression.
•	It is a good practice to break out different joints on each trip, giving the crew an opportunity to look at each pin and box every two or three trips. Inspect the shoulders for galls and possible washouts. Thread protectors should be used when laying down drill collars (Drill pipe also). Periodically, based on drilling conditions and experience, a magnetic particle inspection should be performed. Before storing the drill collars they should be cleaned, and if necessary, reface shoulders with a shoulder refacing tool, "fins" on shoulders beveled, and a good rust preventative applied to the connections. Install thread protectors.

2.1.3	SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR DIFFERENT TYPES OF BHA'S
•	DIRECTIONAL DRILLING: Consider short drill collars to "lock in " the desired target direction. Directional "Locked Up" assemblies permit the use of optimum weights and rotary speeds in directionally drilled holes. The desired hole angle and direction is locked -in with the stiff bottom hole assembly. Proper wall support makes the speed of directional drilling approach the speed of straight hole drilling. Rig efficiency is increased by: Improving bit efficiency with stabilization, more footage per bit, faster penetration rates; Minimizing dogleg severity; Increasing course length; Requiring fewer bottom hole assemblies; Reducing the possibility of differential sticking. This assembly consists of two 15' short drill collars with a non-magnetic drill collar between them. Sufficient stiffness is provided to minimize change in hole angle and direction, yet flexible enough to pass through the "kick-off" point. This assembly allows the more efficient rotation of the drill string either by top drive or rotary
•	USE OF HWDP: Excessive drill collar connection failures result from collars bending as they rotate through doglegs and hole angle changes. Drill collars lay on the low side of high angle holes. This results in: A. Increased rotary torque. B. Increased possibility of differential sticking. C. Increased vertical drag. D. Excessive wall friction that creates rolling action and affects directional control. Heavy-Wate drill pipe bends in the tube not in the connections. This reduces the likely hood of tool joint failure as it rotates through doglegs and hole angle changes. It also offers less wall contact area between the pipe and hole wall which results in; Less rotary torque, Less chance of differential sticking, Less vertical drag, The driller can maintain better directional control. The assembly usually consists of a short BHA. (2-3 drill collars either with a motor or with out, non-magnetic drill collar, mule shoe or MWD) followed with about 21 Heavy-Wate drill pipe, and any stabilizers needed. 
•	CONSIDERATIONS FOR DIFFERENTIAL STICKING: Spiral or grooved drill collars and drill pipe. These tools present a smaller contact area with the wall of the hole. The spiral also allows fluid passage and equalizing of borehole pressures around the collars or pipe. Heavy-Wate drill pipe, the tool joints at the ends and the integral upset in the center of the tube act as centralizer's to hold the heavy wall tube sections of the wall of the hole. Stabilizers, positioned throughout the drilling string, are another positive way of preventing differential sticking. Rotating blade, welded blade, and non-rotating sleeve type stabilizers are used to keep the drill collars centered in the hole. Selection of the type of stabilizers and their spacing varies with the formation being drilled, the size of hole, etc. 
•	HWDP IN TRANSITION: Many drill pipe failures occur in the drill string because of fatigue damage previously accumulated when the failed joint of pipe was run directly above the drill collars. This accelerated fatigue damage is attributed to the bending stress concentration in the limber drill pipe rotating next to the stiff drill collars. Two factors that cause extreme bending stress concentration in the bottom joint of drill pipe are: (1) Cyclic torsional whipping that moves down through the rotating drill pipe into the stiff drill collars. (2) Side to side movement as well as the vertical bounce and vibrations of the drill collars that are transmitted up to the joint of drill pipe. When drill pipe is subjected to compressive buckling, these stress concentrations are much more severe. Many drillers periodically move the bottom joint of drill pipe to a location higher up in the drill pipe string. Moving these joints to other string locations does not remove the cumulative fatigue damage that has been done.
•	Heavy-Wate drill pipe is an intermediate weight drill string member with a tube wall approximately 1" thick. This compares to approximately 3/8" wall thickness for regular drill pipe and approximately 2" wall thickness for drill collars. Heavy-Wate provides a graduated change in stiffness between the limber drill pipe above and the rigid drill collars below. This graduated change in stiffness reduces the likelihood of drill pipe fatigue failures when Heavy-Wate drill pipe is run in the critical transition zone between drill pipe and drill collars. Records over the past few years, proves that the fatigue failure rate is reduced. The H W Drill pipe can be handled like normal pipe and also can be inspected by the same technique used for checking drill collars. The number of joints that should be run in the transition zone is important. Based on successful field experience, 3 to 6 stands of Heavy-Wate drill pipe are recommended between the drill collars and drill pipe.  
•	REAMERS: In an Inclined hole, unsupported drill collars tend to lie on the low side of the hole. When a reamer (stabilizer) is used just above the bit, it becomes a pivot point with collars above exerting a force toward the low side of the hole, and thus pushing the bit toward the high side of the hole. Directional drillers use this technique to build hole angle. To avoid an increase of hole angle where a reamer is necessary, Use a stabilizer at the top of the first drill collar above the reamer. This will align the forces on the bit with the axis of the hole, and will minimize changes in angle.

2.2	BIT CHECKLIST
•	Confirm correct bit delivery to the docks.
•	Ensure delivery of bits and accessories against manifest and rig inventory.  Check container labelling against manifest.
•	Ascertain that delivered bits comply with any special well plan requirements.
•	Check stamped API pin on each bit against container label and manifest.  Including size, bit type, serial number and assembly number.
•	Visually inspect threads, proper bit pin, size and type and box or pin-up.
•	Make certain all required bit breakers are on board.
•	Look down into the bit to check for any possible debris that could cause flow obstruction.  Also visually check for possible cracks or other defects.
•	Ensure O-rings are seated properly in the nozzle bore, feel them for any defects.  (A very light application of petroleum jelly can be applied, to protect the O-ring from the elements if desired).
•	For roller cone bits, especially sealed bits, ensure that the cones do not rotate freely (they should be difficult if not virtually impossible to rotate).
•	Check inserts on Roller Cones for integrity.
•	For PDC bits a small RUBBER mallet can be used to LIGHTLY strike cutters to ensure braze integrity
•	Check gage rings for proper sizes, GO and NO-GO gages.
•	Check for proper nozzles, these will typically have part numbers associated with the bit size ranges.  For roller cone bits place the nozzle in the bore to ensure the right size (it is not necessary to seat the nozzle on the O-ring).  For any threaded nozzles inspect the threads on the nozzles, and screw the nozzle into the bit to ensure no problems (again it is not necessary to seat the nozzle).
•	Check for proper nozzle installation tools, including nails, hammer, snap rings, snap ring pliers and loctite for threaded nozzles if recommended (blue coded loctite is typically used)
•	Make certain nozzle gages are onboard and gage all nozzles.
•	Check for specification sheets for the bits.
•	Place proper GO gage ring on bit to ensure bit is in gage.
•	Make certain that the bit is properly jetted (recheck required TFA) prior to installation on rig floor.

2.3	CASING RUNNING CHECKLIST

2.3.1	PROPER LOAD-OUT OF CASING AT DOCKS OR STAGING AREA
•	Compare the casing program against the equipment received: thread types, length, size (OD & ID, drift), grades & weights.
•	Ensure that threads are properly treated and protected prior to loading.
•	Ensure proper space-out pup joints with the appropriate threads and crossovers for landing casing are available.
•	Confirm overall casing delivery quantities against measured setting depth (MD) before the casing leaves the vendor's yard.  Include excess pipe.
•	Verify that chrome casing is in rack or otherwise suitable for transport.
•	Verify proper casing handling materials is at the dockside for transferring casing from trucks to workboat. (Slings, cranes, etc.) 
•	Specify delivery requirements for loading out the workboat.  Specify which joints are first in the hole to facilitate correct order of loading.
•	Confirm pick-up criteria with the wellsite, e.g. threads to bow, or stern as required, along with any special loading instructions for sandblasted or externally coated/treated casing.  Ensure information is provided for special threads handling.
•	Ensure visual inspection of all tubulars for condition and integrity.
•	Ensure proper delivery papers are in order, including inspection criteria as required. (QA)
•	Inspect casing head and associated equipment.
•	Confirm proper sizing for all casing head hardware, casing hangers and associated equipment.
•	Ensure that the rig has proper number of drifts for all sizes & weights of casing, if not ordered with casing.
•	Confirm that all necessary certifications are in hand, i.e. lifting, conformity, pressure testing.

2.3.2	PRE-RUNNING CHECKS
•	Conduct Tool Box safety meeting for safe off-loading and handling of casing.
•	Inspect racking area, variable deck load requirements and limitations within the area (i.e., point loading), proper and safe racking materials, shims, etc
•	Inspect crane, harness, and all handling equipment.
•	Confirm crane certification and operator qualifications.
•	Inspect casing head and associated equipment.
•	Ensure proper sizing for all casing, casing head equipment, casing hangers, pack-offs and associated equipment.
•	Review any special equipment requirements including bales, Tam packer, Reagan FSOC etc.
•	Ensure casing is properly and safely restrained.
•	Unload, number, and tally measurements for all tubulars, obtaining two independent tallies for verification purposes. Specify how the tally is to be obtained. (i.e. measured from where to where.)
•	Make a casing running list.  It should clearly state which joint numbers are to be run and left out.  Total each case.
•	Confirm weights, grades and drift of casing or liners.
•	When possible, drift with largest diameter tool anticipated to be inserted into the casing at its minimum ID.  For example, drift with actual bit verification within the heaviest joint of casing, i.e. smallest ID.
•	Ensure proper subs and handling tools have been received.  Ensure all have recent inspections.
•	Ensure casing with centralizers or other hardware will pass thru spider slips.
•	Record, monitor and tag any special, non-mag, radioactive or other "Tagged" joints of casing.
•	Confirm proper placement of special joints of casing (sandblasted, externally coated, etc.)
•	Review any well plan shoe track requirements.
•	Install float equipment with shoe track, thread lock as appropriate.  Float collar should be on pin end.
•	Install centralizers on pipe rack if possible.
•	In close tolerance holes, install centralizers between stop collars or between stop collar and casing coupling.
•	Ensure safe access/egress to casing when racked has been planned.
•	Confirm a steam cleaner (preferable option) or high-pressure washer is available for thread cleaning.  Ensure system of catching run off is implemented to avoid environmental problems.
•	Ensure a sufficient quantity of 2 X 4's (not 4 X 4's) dunnage is available to strip casing.  Minimum of 4 per row.  Six if 20” or better.
•	Inspect the casing internally / externally for corrosion / rust, and issue a report.
•	Slings and handling tools for backloading at rig site.
•	If chrome casing, ensure that suitable canvas slings are available for handling.
•	Drift tools for casing string, hangers and work string as appropriate.
•	Pickup and lay down equipment as required.
•	Proper cementation aids:
1.	Casing scraper (s), as required
2.	Special requirements such as: centralizers, stand-offs, turbolizers, stop rings, etc.
3.	Sufficient personnel to complete the casing running operations.
4.	Confirm functioning float equipment and cementing equipment when required. Refer to: Cementing Casing Checklists
5.	Confirm type of float equipment; shoe, collars (type - rotatable, non-rotatable, PDC drillable, etc.)
6.	Wiper plugs launcher as required for cementing head and associated equipment.
7.	Connection thread - lock materials to make up ahead of time, in town or on pipe rack.)
8.	Install float collar on pin end of casing joint.
•	Install centralizers on pipe racks.
•	Conduct verifications of required personnel and handling tools for running casing.
•	Consider strongly have a spear on location when running casing.
•	Function test torque assurance equipment to ensure proper makeup.
•	Have casing thread X 2" Weco thread half circulating swage and a casing thread X 4 1/2" IF circulating swage on hand on the drill floor.
•	Service company verification of any special landing/testing tools.
•	Ensure back up casing running tools are on location, inspected, and operational prior to commencing running casing.
•	Don't forget to pull the wear bushing.

2.3.3	RUNNING CASING
•	Conduct Toolbox safety meeting for all involved rig personnel.
•	Communicate circulation and conditioning requirements with appropriate personnel.
•	Review hole stability before tripping to run casing. Refer to Tripping Checklists
•	Confirm whether bottom hole location survey is required, prior to POH to run casing.
•	Check the following before coming out of hole to run casing:
1.	What is the buoyed hook load of the casing and the block?
2.	How much over pull will be allowed to reciprocate the pipe or to pull free if stuck?
3.	How many lines will be needed for hook load requirements?
4.	What is the load rating of the structure and derrick?
5.	Will some of the BHA need to be laid down to allow the overpull allowed above?
•	Make-up cement head to landing joint.  Review operations and pressure rating criteria.
•	If sub-sea, ensure the following:
1.	Ensure proper crossover from sub sea running tool to sub sea plug launcher.
2.	Make-up casing hanger, plug launching equipment, running tool then stand it back in the derrick on a landing string.
3.	Make-up the top drive cementing head c/w plug releasing dart then stand it back in the derrick.
4.	Drift ALL tubulars in the landing string, including the running tools to a greater diameter of the pump-down plug-releasing dart.
5.	Check for hold down pins in prior wellhead section and remove wear bushing if present.
6.	Ensure that the running string is of sufficient strength to handle casing weight.
•	Confirm that appropriate casing rams are installed and tested.
•	Function and test all BOPE, as required.
•	Review rig maintenance and ensure that cutting and slipping drill line will not be necessary while running casing.
•	Ascertain whether torque gauges are calibrated properly and are the correct gauge for tongs being utilized.  Differential pressure readings are not adequate.
•	Torque turn or equivalent should be utilized on premium thread connections.
•	Check running-in-hole displacement criteria
•	Review makeup torque, special threads requirements.
•	Communicate sections of hole instability and have work instructions in place before running casing. 
•	Prepare special instructions, as necessary, for running procedures; possible shallow gas, kill operations, etc.
•	Mark all weight and grades in proper running sequence.
•	Ensure proper handling tools on the rig floor, and make any necessary risk assessments.
•	Ensure stabbing board is in place properly and safe.
•	Confirm proper calibration and rig-up of all test & torque verification equipment.
•	Communicate swab and surge criteria to all appropriate personnel.
•	Inspect trip tanks, mud tank levels and condition.  Verify operation of transfer pumps.
•	Communicate and post maximum over pull allowed on casing during running or pulling operations.
•	Ensure proper doping techniques are utilized.  Consider using a paintbrush.
•	Use thread locking compound on both pin and coupling threads of all float equipment and stage cementing equipment.
•	Inspect all handling devices for picking up and running of casing.
•	Clamp-on or inflatable thread protectors are recommended.
•	Communicate the importance of all thread makeup and alignment to minimize thread damage. Make up casing to API recommended optimum torque (or as per drilling program). Torque to be not less than specified minimum torque and not more than specified maximum torque.  Use of stabbing guide is recommended.
•	Check float operation on initial insertion into the hole.
•	Fill-up casing a minimum of every 5 joints, unless planned to float in of casing.
•	If float in casing, insure casing and float shoe/collar differential are adequate.
•	Communicate wash down criteria, if any, along with circulating and conditioning (staging) criteria while running in the hole.
•	Prior to picking up landing joint, check casing count and/or drill pipe count to ensure proper depth.
•	Check length of land joint to be sure the collar of the preceding joint can be set below the casing head flange while still leaving adequate pipe above the Kelly bushing to clear elevators.
•	Communicate landing criteria to appropriate personnel.  Unless notified otherwise, land casing in wellhead in the position in which it was cemented (i.e., land casing with tension-string weights). The slips will be set in the wellhead so that all of the casing weight is transferred to the slips without picking up or working the pipe
•	Review circulating and conditioning criteria before cementing.

2.3.4	REFERENCE
  Well Control while running Casing (Well Control Manual)

2.4	CEMENTING CASING CHECKLIST

2.4.1	GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS
•	Review the program to ensure requirements are understood and obtainable.
•	Lower mud gel strength, yield point, and plastic viscosity to within value range specified before removing the drill pipe.  Be careful not to impair mud's ability to suspend the weighting agent.
•	Clean and inspect all bulk tanks prior to blowing cement in.
•	Confirm thickening time sufficient for the job requirements.
•	Ensure cement & water samples are forwarded in adequate time for testing. Review written results as soon as available w/ engineering & service company
•	Tanks used for mix water should be clean.
•	Ensure actual mix water temperature is within design parameter
•	Verify stand back area boards are in good condition.
•	Verify rig equipment adequate for the pumping rate & pressure rating.
•	Confirm tubing and handling tools are on location for possible top job.
•	Verify rig handling capacities, slings, spreader bars, space for tubulars, sufficient Chicksans & lines are fit for purpose.
•	Confirm pipe movement effects - check V-door tail rope for length, defects, etc. Replace if necessary.
•	Check preventive maintenance for permanently installed equipment.
•	Confirm cementing lines secured, flushed & tested
•	Ensure stage collar, first stage plug, opening bomb, and closing plug are on location prior to pull out of hole with drill pipe (if stage cement job is planned).  Confirm that all are fit for purpose.
•	Correct stinger, cementing head & plugs, equipment for running retainer (connections for cementing kelly, top drive side swivel, or power swivel).
1.	Proper connections
2.	Both top and bottom plugs are correct size and compatible for seating and sealing.
3.	Plugs are compatible with bit to be utilized on drill out.
•	Ensure that Surface Dart Launcher and SST are checked and loaded.
1.	Proper connections
2.	Both top and bottom plugs are correct size and compatible for seating and sealing.
3.	Plugs are compatible with bit to be utilized on drill out.
•	Have a good  (at least 3” hose) with ¼ turn valve running off a dedicated centrifugal pump to fill casing.
•	If the pipe is to be reciprocated during the job, ensure the air hoist is installed on the chiksans/cementing hose to assist in raising and lowering.
•	Prior to loading cement, ensure tanks are clean and free of moisture.  Ensure all spiders (air jets) are clean and in good shape.
•	Prior to taking cement from boats, ensure all hoses are in good shape.  Blow air through to tanks prior to blowing any bulk.  Ensure no obstructions.
•	Check back-up equipment (if/when available) for bulk air compressor, mix water lines/pumps, etc.
•	Ensure provisions have been made to handle displaced fluids during cementing job (lower mud tank levels if needed).
•	Safety Sub: Have a X-over from the casing thread to the drill pipe
•	Ensure Safety Sub ID is full opening & OD will allow casing to be run to bottom without clearance problems.  Ensure top of X-over is beveled to allow ease of pulling out of hole.
•	Verify sufficient cement & additives for casing job & squeeze work, if required
•	Use cement unit for displacing, i.e. using a barrel counter and a marked tank for cross check
•	Verify that there are proper plans for all related transportation matters, such as cement barge, workboats, and helicopters.
•	Be sure that dry air is always used to handle cement.
•	Verify cement location i.e. silos, permanent tanks, multiple tanks.
•	Be prepared to handle the large mud volume that will be displaced from the hole during the cementing operation.  Plan for disposal or storage.
•	Collect a sample (a minimum of 1 gallon) from each compartment of each bulk tanks.  Collect a minimum of 2 gallons of the mix water for the same purpose.
•	Review hole geometry and mud properties to determine potential for problems.
•	Prior to the job, review the requirement for additional personnel needed such as toolhands, field engineer, cementing supervisor, helpers, casing crews, mud engineer, etc.
•	Discuss and develop contingencies with well site team: Do you need a wiper trip after logging? What action will be taken if: some of the major pumping is lost, lose returns, early returns, inadequate density maintenance, plug doesn't bump, stage cementing tools fails to open, casing doesn't reach bottom, floats don't hold, backside flow after job, casing parts or splits, plugged floats, lost foreign material, weather.
•	Ensure that there are at least two independent mixing & displacement calculations for comparison.
•	Liquid Additive Systems (L.A.S.). Check and understand the type system to be used.	Refer to: LAS Checklists
•	Confirm correct volumes & sufficient quantity (excess if required) of mix water additives, cement, pit volume, plugs, spacers, and displacement fluids.
•	Recheck volume & temperature determinations for the job. It is very important to have very accurate displacement calculations
•	If drill pipe is used as a landing string, use the tooljoint corrected volumes.
•	Well site Supervisor & Cementer must prepare & compare independent mixing & displacement calculations.
•	Ensure that the program volumes been adjusted based on the caliper information. 
•	Verify the following cementing calculations:
1.	Weight of casing prior to landing & equipment safety factors.
2.	Weight of casing at the hanger when cement is in place.
3.	Landing string buoyed weight when SSR in use.
4.	Circulation volume.
5.	Volume and density of preflush.
6.	Reduction, if any, of hydrostatic head due to preflush, assuming gauge hole.  Ensure sufficient overbalance is maintained.
7.	Lead & tail slurry volume including excess
8.	Hydrostatic pressure when cement fully displaced.  Check against expected frac pressure @ shoe or weakest zone.
9.	Mix water, cement, & additives requirements for all slurries. 
10.	Minimum slurry volumes ignoring excesses.
11.	Capacity of cement lines & displacement volumes
12.	Time for bottom plug to bump.
13.	Slurry mixing time.
14.	Displacement time & strokes.
15.	Total job time compared to thickening time.
16.	Anticipated and maximum (if gauge) TOC.
17.	Calculate the maximum tension applied to top of casing when plug bumps & when casing is pressure tested.  The values are theoretical, and actual well conditions must be monitored.
•	Confirm the following calculations (Cementing program & cementing engineer):
1.	Theoretical pumping pressures versus slurry & displacement volumes.
2.	Maximum possible returns from the cement job, ignoring circulating losses, and the maximum displacement volume required to catch up with "U" tubed cement.
3.	Theoretical static differential pressure to bump plug.
•	Maximum circulating & displacing rates which will not cause the ECD to exceed formation allowable pressures.
•	Ensure good communications between the rig floor and the cementing unit.
•	Perform Pre Job Safety Meeting.
•	Collect samples of cement, blend, and mixed water.
•	If displacing with rig pumps, test pump efficiency prior to the cement job.
•	Verify specifications for mud properties prior to cementing.
•	Verify criteria for reciprocating casing during cement job.
•	Ensure that the mudloggers monitor for returns during job, reporting losses, gases, etc, to the Driller, Mud Engineer, Drilling Supervisor.
•	Ensure to lower closing pressure for annular preventer for casing as per the chart.
•	Consider using the actual ID of the casing, need to micrometer, for displacement volumes, especially for 9 5/8" casing.

2.4.2	JOB EXECUTION
•	Collect a dry sample during the job when 15-20%, 45-50%, and 80-85% of the cement being used for a total of 3 samples minimum.  Collect a liquid additive samples prior to job and mix water w/ additives during job.  The cementing contractor should advise on the amount of sample required.
•	The Mud Engineer shall record initial pit levels. He shall be present at the mud pits during the whole cementing and displacement operations reporting any loss on returns, pertinent facts and data.
•	Ensure to take samples of the mixed cement at the START, MIDDLE and END of each stage (lead & tail) of the cement job.
•	Use pressurized balances & ensure that they were calibrated.
•	Verify loading order of plugs and integrity of bottom plug. Note on the job log.
•	Verify two plug cementing head for proper operation and special bail requirements, if required.  The Cementing Operator shall personally release the top plug and the Well Site Supervisor shall personally witness the process.
•	If a two plug head is unavailable, load the top plug into the head after kicking out the bottom plug with pre-flush.
•	Ensure adequate spacer mixing facilities on the rig.
•	If using stab-in tools be careful to prevent casing collapse should hole bridge off.
•	Surface lines should be manifolded in advance to facilitate plug release without having to break connections to drop plugs.
•	Consult design to confirm order of additives for addition to mix water or cement.
•	Ensure that every effort is made to keep the air dry for blowing the cement.
•	Prepare for cement returns to surface and determine in advance where to put it.
•	Do not pull the bulk tank(s) dry
•	Confirm that the top plug will be displaced until it bumps (or over-displacement volume established).
•	Upon displacement of the top plug, test casing after the floats have been checked.  Record final pressure just prior to bumping plug to determine TOC.
•	After landing the top plug, check for backflow.  If none occurs, leave the casing open and flow check for 30 min.  If the float doesn't hold, close the casing, but monitor pressure build-up until the cement sets up.  Do not let the pressure build-up in the casing exceed the differential pressure required to displace the plug to the float collar (the maximum surface pressure just before the plug bumps).
•	Upon conclusion, measure & record cement and mix water volumes.  Use material balance calculations to confirm cement was pumped as designed.  Make note of differences on job report.
•	The job is not finished until all reporting has been completed!

2.4.3	GENERAL REFERENCES
Cement Slurry Design
Cement Placement
Setting and Verification of Cement Plugs
Leak Off Test or Shoe Test

2.5	CEMENT PLUG CHECKLIST

Ensure that you are familiar with Setting and Verification of Cement Plugs

General Considerations

Equipment Requirements & Limitations	Personnel Physical Condition

2.5.1	GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS
•	Review the program to ensure requirements are understood and obtainable.
•	Verify the program obtains the results as it is written. Confirm thickening time sufficient for the job requirements.
•	Design plug as balanced plug.  Insure that spacers will not contribute to well control problems.
•	Ensure swab/surge calculations have been performed and reviewed with rig personnel. Know fracture gradient limitations.
•	Determine if there are any thief zones.
•	Determine if reverse circulation is possible.
•	Ensure cement & water samples are forwarded in adequate time for testing. Review written results as soon as available with engineering & service company.
•	Verify rig equipment is adequate for the pumping rate & pressure rating.
•	Verify rig handling capacities, slings, spreader bars, space for tubulars, sufficient Chicksans & lines that are fit for purpose.
•	Confirm cementing lines secured & tested.
•	Correct stinger, cementing head & plugs, equipment for running retainer (connections for cementing kelly, top drive side swivel, or power swivel).
•	Check back-up equipment (if/when available) for bulk air compressor, mix water lines/pumps, etc.
•	Verify sufficient cement & additives for the cement plug (s).
•	Verify cement location i.e. silos, permanent tanks, multiple tanks.
•	Evaluate economics for personnel & equipment standby costs vs. rig time savings.
•	Review hole geometry and mud properties to determine potential for problems
•	Prior to the job, review requirements for additional personnel needed such as toolhands, field engineer, cementing supervisor, helpers, mud engineer, etc.
•	Develop contingencies: action that will be taken if cement sets prematurely, stuck DP, equipment fails, unexpected pressures, to be discussed at the Toolbox meeting.
•	Liquid Additive Systems (LAS). Check and understand the type system to be used.  Refer to: LAS Checklists.

2.5.2	TUBULARS
•	Clean, tally, and inspect tubulars. Verify tubular weights and actual dimensions for displacement and capacity volumes.
•	Check Power/Backup tongs.
•	Ensure to rabbit work string. Ensure rabbit is hollow and has a 100', or appropriate length, steel line.
•	Use stingers when laying balanced plugs.  The length of the stinger should exceed the height of the cement plug by 1.5 times.  Stinger should be tubing with centralizers and diverter sub.  If diverter sub is unavailable bull plug or orange peel bottom joint of pipe and cut holes of minimum of ½” diameter evenly spaced in bottom joint of tubing.  Expendable tubing (aluminum or fiber glass) should be considered for stinger in deep, hot holes.
•	Run Gray Inside BOP in string.  Position for IBOP to be at rig floor when bottom of stinger clears cement plug on pull out.
•	Pipe movement: Rotate whenever possible to enhance cement job. Consider the following;
1.	If pipe movement is not possible, consideration should be given to mechanical aids (turbulators, etc)
2.	Begin pipe movement immediately and continue through cementing.
3.	Begin pipe movement before or during circulation prior to cementing if equipment will allow and continue through cementing.
•	Verify rotation procedures.

2.5.3	JOB EXECUTION
•	Ensure that a Toolbox meeting is held with the relevant personnel to discuss the operational and safety issues related to the job.
•	Operationally, setting a plug follows the same procedure as a primary cement job, except the drillstring/workstring is used in place of casing.
•	Visually inspect all tanks to be used for cleanliness.
•	Confirm correct volumes & sufficient quantity (excess if required) of mix water additives, cement, pit volume, plugs, spacers, displacement fluids.
•	Recheck volume & temperature determinations for the job. It is very important to have very accurate displacement calculations.
•	Ensure that the program volumes been adjusted based on the caliper information. 
•	Wellsite Supervisor & Cementer must prepare & compare independent mixing & displacement calculations.
•	Spot 100 linear foot viscous pill immediately below anticipate bottom of cement plug if open hole or fish below (weight of viscous pill to match mud weight or be as close as possible.
•	Verify the following cementing calculations:
1.	Weight of all tubulars before, during and after cementing.
2.	Circulation volume.
3.	Volume and density of preflush.
4.	Reduction of hydrostatic head due to preflush. Ensure overbalance is maintained as desired.
5.	Cement slurry volume including excess.
6.	Hydrostatic pressure when cement fully displaced. Check against expected frac pressure of formation.
7.	Mix water, cement, & additives requirements for all slurry
8.	Capacity of cement lines & displacement volumes.  Use tool joint corrected values for DP displacement.
9.	Theoretical pumping pressures versus slurry & displacement volumes.
10.	Maximum possible returns, ignoring circulating losses, and the maximum displacement volume required to catch up with "U" tubed cement.
11.	Slurry mixing time.
12.	Displacement time.
13.	Total job time compared to thickening time.
14.	Theoretical static differential pressure.
15.	Anticipated TOC
•	Ensure good communications between the rig floor and the cementing unit.
•	Collect samples of cement, blend, and mixed water.
•	Verify slurry density with pressurized balances.
•	Maintain an operations record or log of pertinent job information for company reporting (i.e., DIMS).
•	Confirm batch mixing, if possible. Avoid using jet mixers. Alternatively, use recirculating type mixers or equivalent continuous mix systems with auto density control.
•	If Liquid Additive Systems are used, identify as gravity feed or electronic control. Refer to : LAS Checklists.
•	Ensure the mix water is clean, salinity and temperature is within design parameter.
•	Liquid additives may be mixed in the cement displacement tanks if prejob mixing is not possible. Dry additives must be premixed prior to the job.
•	Check the bulk delivery rate to ensure sufficient feed rate.  Monitor the ventline to ensure the cement is not being discharged through it.
•	Dryness of supply air is an important factor.
•	During the job, check the volumes pumped using material balance computations against the barrel counters on the cementing unit.
•	Do not pull the bulk tank(s) dry.
•	Upon conclusion verify cement & mix water volumes using material balance to confirm cement was pumped as designed
•	Verify whether operation will reverse out or circulate conventionally in advance of operation. If to circulate out conventionally, may need to increase thickening time.
•	Follow design procedure for waiting on cement. Complete all company & regulatory reporting, immediately.
•	Verify plug is set, for sidetracking may not be done with kick off BHA.

2.6	CLEAN-OUT WELL CHECKLIST 

2.6.1	PLATFORM OPERATION
2.6.1.A	CASING/LINER CLEAN-OUT OPERATION
•	Review program to be sure that the objectives and operation are understood.
•	Ensure that all the equipment required is on board in sufficient time for it to be checked out and not have the operation waiting to pick it up.
•	Hold a Pre-Job Toolbox meeting with all the relevant personnel to discuss the operational and safety issues.
•	Ensure that all the following well information is available:
1.	Casing/liner dimensions (top of liner depth),
2.	Condition of the casing/liner,
3.	Type of fluid in the well,
4.	Status of what might be in the hole i.e. fish, cement, tools, packers, asphaltenes, sand, etc
5.	Status of well i.e. open below casing/liner, obstructions, etc
•	Ensure that the fluid displacements have been organized.  Refer to the Fluids Displacement Checklist.	
•	Check well control equipment is on the rig floor, Gray inside BOP, kelly cock etc, with correct connections.
•	Ensure that hoisting equipment is fit for purpose, functioned and tested, i.e. workover mast, Coiled Tubing Unit or Snubbing Unit.  Refer to the Coiled Tubing Checklist.
•	Ensure that Well Intervention Wellhead is rigged up as per the Rig Up Intervention Tree & Riser Checklist.
•	Verify that the Clean-out BHA is ready prior to running in the hole.  Refer to the BHA Checklist.
•	Ensure the BHA includes a float
•	Check motor condition before RIH. (Bearing gap, ensure bend is zero & the sleeves are torqued up, check the required torques & max flowrates.)
•	Before displacing fluids, function test the fluid pump (manual and auto) with displacement fluid via the displacement line.
•	Function test the alarm panel and ensure it is operative.
•	When displace to OBM, ensure at least 2 mud pumps are primed with mud and ready.
•	Ensure that there is enough working mud volume and tank capacity, if not may have to divert
•	Drill at maximum motor pump rate.
•	Monitor outer annulus during pressure test.
•	Ensure to flow check and circulate bottoms up once drilled through each plug or after tagging plug back TD or top of liner.
•	   Confirm length of flow check needed to accommodate total measured depth and well deviation.

2.6.1.B	LINER
•	Include a float, if not reversing out, and that mill is bored out, to reduce pressure loss and avoid blocking up when reversing.
•	Ensure the circulating sub is functioned, if run.
•	Ensure casing scraper blades face down.
•	Take into consideration thermal contraction when doing weight calculations.
•	Once circulation is established circulate clean making certain return fluids are debris free before beginning displacement.
•	Monitor outer annulus during pressure tests.
•	Consider using dye in spacers to verify diverting requirements.
•	Once liner clean, shear out the SPS & circ BU fast, clean, before displacing to SW, to avoid any debris blocking choke during displacing with Driller's method.  This should be done in a controlled manner, across the choke maintaining backpressure sufficient to balance the well. Once seawater is all round then the choke pressure should be bled off in stages to zero and the well flow checked. Once it is seen that the well is stable then further circulations at high rate should be performed to ensure the well is clean of debris
•	When displacing to Sea Water, consider pumping as fast as possible, if well not perforated yet.  May take ages, if restrict rate to a low level just because you need a lower rate once SW is in annulus 
•	Once liner is reversed to inhibited SW, displace DP to inhib SW chased with 50 bbl of SW, (Cold fluid in DP should avoid backflow and avoid risk to crew.)  Consider using caustic pills followed by high viscosity pills to clean mud from casing walls.
•	Ensure 2 7/8 X 5" X/O is transition type.
•	Do an extra long flowcheck after displacing liner to SW, I.e. if it's an 'S' shaped well. Monitor outer annulus during pressure tests.
•	Have 2 people in derrick for running/pulling 2 7/8" DP.
•	Utilize belly board for 2 7/8” and smaller tubing
•	Consider use of dye in spacers, etc, to help with knowing when to divert etc.

2.7	COILED TUBING CHECKLIST

2.7.1	PRE-JOB CONSIDERATIONS
•	Ensure that program is reviewed and understood and is capable of meeting the programmed objectives.
•	Ensure that the reel history is known and fatigue tracking for coiled tubing is instituted.
•	Have a plan for rig-up. This plan should include:
1.	Ensure that there is sufficient deck space and available variable deck loading allowable.
2.	Roster of who needs to be where and when needed. This involves truckers, rigging people, service hands, crane operators, etc.
3.	Order in which equipment is brought to location
4.	How to communicate between location and staging area (if used) to ensure plan is followed
5.	Which individual is in charge of placing what equipment
6.	The sequence in which equipment assemblies are erected. This applies to wellheads, BOPs, and support structures for the injector. If possible, try to go straight from the trailer to the point of assembly. Place first piece of BOP straight onto wellhead and allow men to start making it up rather than set it aside for later. Then, when second piece arrives, place it straight onto first and so on.
•	Identify the coil tubing application, i.e.:
1.	Clean-out, stimulation, drilling, logging, downhole camera, velocity string, kick-off, production, plunger lift, jet lift, injection, perforating etc.
•	Verify an approved AFE and ensure that all costs are available for cost control.
•	Verify any heavy lifts and crane capacities.
•	Ensure that all regulatory and government permits for the job are on board.
•	Check all safety systems (fire, communications, evacuation plan, gas and H2S detectors, air packs)
•	Check functioning of accumulator unit
1.	Air supply
2.	Fluid reservoir
3.	Regulators
4.	Pump operation
•	Check relief valves on N2 tanks.
•	Ensure to hold a Toolbox meeting to discuss and review the program and safety issues.
•	Ensure that third parties services are notified.
•	Verify that the production personnel (OIM) have been notified to shut in well and/or facilities.
•	Inspect coil-tubing unit prior to mobilization.  Ensure that the coil has been pressure tested.
•	Ensure that all support vessels (barges, supply boats, crew boats, tugs, helicopters) requirements have been identified and available.
•	Check supply of consumable items (fuel, acid, N2, caustic, chemicals) prior to mobilization and/or start of job.
•	Ensure that extra consumables are available to bring out if required.
•	Verify how supplies will be brought to the job site.
•	Verify the pressure rating of pumpers, coil, chicksans, valves, wellhead, choke manifold, choke, blowback lines.
•	Verify the wind direction and how gas will be vented during flow back.
•	Ensure that the coil is drifted before mobilizing the CT unit.
•	Ensure that the CT crew has sufficient experience and training for the equipment.
•	Verify that all the service personnel have the required training certificates (well control, first aid, offshore survival, etc).
•	Verify the availability of chemical cutters and fishing equipment, if needed.
•	Ensure suitable storage and disposal for returned fluids.
•	Verify that the production facilities can handle the returned fluids.
•	Ensure that the well tubing was drifted with slickline prior to rigging up the coil tubing.
•	Ensure that a detailed well schematic is on location.
•	Verify that compatibility tests were run with the reservoir fluids and fluids to be pumped.
•	Ensure that the Xmas tree (master valves, swab valve) was inspected and pressure tested prior to arrival.
•	Ensure that the master valves will hold for the rig up.
•	Verify the connection on the Xmas tree where the CT unit will rig up.  Ensure any crossovers or spools are on location prior to rig up.
•	Verify the weather forecast prior to rig up.
•	Confirm whether H2S is expected in the formation fluids.
•	Ensure that locks and chains available to lockout the master valves when coil is in the hole.
•	Ensure sufficient chains and securing equipment are available to stabilize the injector after deployment.
•	Ensure preparation is made to do a free point calculation.
•	Verify the minimum ID in the wellbore and at the target depth.
•	Confirm if there is any scale, paraffin, asphaltenes, or hydrates expected in the wellbore.
•	Review the well history available showing past work.  Ensure to draw a wellbore status schematic if not available.
•	Verify the pressure expected.
•	Ensure that the fluid tanks were cleaned prior to loading fluids.
•	Verify the existence of a backup coiled tubing reel near to the operation.
•	Ensure that the DHSV is open before RIH.  Check it by pumping through and bleeding off, etc.  Ensure that there is a sleeve available to lock it open.
•	Visually check the BHA for possible non-chamfered edges.
•	Ensure that there are suitable access steps.
•	Verify if a flag / marker is required for the coil.

2.7.2	EQUIPMENT CONSIDERATIONS
•	Ensure the following is available:
1.	A range of drift balls available and more than one of each size.
2.	A round file available for filing down the internal bead prior to installing the connector.
3.	Sample containers for catching samples of fluids pumped and returned.
4.	Litmus paper for checking pH.
5.	Coil life data available for coil (number of cycles and splices)
6.	Backups available for all items in BHA.
7.	A CoilCade calculation is made to predict tubing buckling characteristics.
•	Check the coil for an internal seam or bead that reduces the effective ID.
•	Check OD of ball for activating the safety disconnect and the circulating valve.
•	Check the size, weight, grade, and length of coil.
•	Verify the associated equipment will be used with the service company, i.e.:
1.	Mills, bits, motors, disconnector, circulating valve, jars, accelerators, centralizers, connector and tools to install connector, knuckle or swivel joints, fishing tools (coil cutter, overshot, spear, alligator grab), hangers, double flapper check valve, washing / jetting tools, perforator, running / pulling for nipples, shifting tools, tubing end locator, cement valve, venturi junk basket, bailers, drifts, impression blocks, production logging tools, etc.
•	Verify the capacities and pressure ratings of fluid pumps, N2 pumps for suitability.
•	Confirm whether N2 is needed to blow the coil dry after the operation.
•	On a semi operation, verify the spaceout and height of the frame for rig up.
•	Verify the operating parameters of the downhole motors and other equipment.
•	Function all BOPs to ensure proper hookup
•	Pressure test BOPs and all lines per program
•	Function test mud pumps, solids equipment, manifolding, etc.
•	Stab tubing into injector and install injector atop support structure. Make up to BOP and test
•	Work all CT functions
•	Pump all around system (pumps through reel, through BOP, choke/kill lines/manifold and back to pits)
•	Check that all CT instrumentation is functioning (CTSI, footage counters, etc)
•	Check that support system/hydraulics for injector and support structure is functioning properly
•	Check that well control instrumentation (flow-meter/pit-level floats, etc) is working and alarms understood.
•	Check that all gas detectors are working

2.7.3	RUNNING GUIDELINES
•	Extreme care should be exercised while running the BHA through the Xmas tree, wellhead assembly and restrictions (landing nipples, gas lift mandrels etc). The running speed through these areas should not exceed 10 ft/min.
•	Make several pull tests while RIH. Pull test may be spaced on uniform tubing sections. Choose depths for pull tests in agreement with CT Engineer or Supervisor. Call for a pull test before entering into a new tubing / liner section; before passing through restriction in the tubing/liner; before tagging TD or fill. Compare actual and predicted readings and adjust factor on CoilCADE.
•	Initial runs into a well should exercise extreme caution and running speeds should not exceed 60 ft/min. In circumstances where numerous runs are made, the speed may be increased once the well characteristics have been established. The maximum running or pulling speed should never exceed 100 ft/min.
•	Running speed through tubing restrictions (DHSVs, gas lift mandrels, nipples, WLEGs, tailpipe, etc) should be reduced to 25 ft/min.
•	If a restriction is encountered and the coiled tubing has hung up, the coiled tubing shall be pulled back the required amount to expose the tubing immediately below the injector chains for inspection.
•	If the coiled tubing becomes hung up while POOH, the maximum over pull shall not exceed 80 per cent of the yield strength of the coiled tubing taper which is across the injector head chains, and to a point 1000 ft below the chains. Attention should be paid at all times to the taper across the injector head chains and any taper changes that occur close to surface. If there is a lighter taper within approximately 1000 feet of surface, the yield of this taper should be used as the limit point for over-pull.
•	In circumstances of pressurized or flowing wells, the pressure inside the coiled tubing shall be maintained at all times in order to prevent collapse.
•	Pull tests shall be performed at 1000 foot intervals noting the RIH and POOH weight indicator readings. On multiple runs, it has been noted that the pull tests should be conducted over different intervals of the coiled tubing to avoid over-cycling one area. Typically. Compare the weight reading with the simulated weight in TFM (Tubing Forces Module).
•	If the operation involves tagging a known obstruction, the running speed should be reduced to 10 ft/min approximately 50 feet above the known obstruction depth. The obstruction should be tagged with extreme care with the minimum weight being set down.
•	Any depth correlation or obstructions shall be flagged on the coiled tubing with a paint mark, and the flag recorded on the service report.
•	Ensure geometry monitoring of CT to locate mechanical damage, necking and/or ballooning of the CT.
•	Ensure CT cycling at butt welds is avoided.
•	Ensure that before repairing or replacing the power unit, the following steps are taken:
1.	Close the slip rams and the pipe rams. Close the manual locks on both sets of rams in case of hydraulic leakage.
2.	If applicable, apply the reel brake.
3.	Maintain circulation as required (e.g. to prevent tubing collapse or the settling of well bore solids).

2.7.4	DRILLING OPERATIONS
•	Hold pre-tour meetings with all personnel coming on tour to communicate exactly what is going on, what to look out for, and what is to happen during the next 12 hours.
•	Allow for 10-15 minutes minimum handover for ALL personnel before departing crews leave
•	After handover, visit briefly with each crewmember to ensure they are comfortable with everything happening
•	Anticipate unusual events and be physically on hand to help monitor and explain to personnel. (Such as, drilling break, gas increase, bottoms up samples, weighting mud)
•	As often as required (at least once per tour) visit with CTD driller and discuss what he would do "if" … develop various scenarios. Most CTD drillers do not fully understand the shutting in of wells. They are used to different types of BOPs and procedures.
•	Do a "walk-around at least once per tour to visually inspect the location and all equipment. Use this to help train the personnel in what you are doing, what you are looking for, and what to do if something is wrong.
•	Create JSAs for any task that is being done for the first time (with these personnel, virtually every one will be their "first time". Conduct lots of toolbox safety meetings prior to such tasks.
•	Keep on top of mud system – Due to low flow rates, hole cleaning is critical with CTD. Constantly check for returns by estimating volume of cuttings generated and comparing to actual volume across shakers/cleaning system. 
•	Best procedure for avoiding cuttings buildup in deviated wells is to work the tubing and circulate. Short trips should be frequent and as needed based on returns and observations of other indicators (down hole instrumentation, drag, etc.) Typically, a short trip will cover the section of new hole just drilled (after, say 50-75 ft in 4 ¾”) and then all the way back to the shoe/window/last dogleg after 2-3 short trips (depending on hole conditions and mud performance/cuttings returns). Consult with the Directional Driller on this.
•	Supervise the handling of all tubulars and BHA handling; at least until you are sure personnel are familiar with the use of slips, tongs, etc.
•	Supervise the testing of BOPs

2.8	CONDUCTOR RUNNING CHECKLIST

2.8.1	PREPARATION TO RUNNING CONDUCTOR
•	Refer to the drilling program for any relevant instructions and comments.
•	Ensure that the correct 3rd party personnel are on board.
•	Prepare an equipment list for the anticipated operation.
•	Ensure that all equipment is the correct size and in good working order.
•	Ensure that permits are in place for heavy lifts. Inform the OIM of heavy lifts, including the timing.  If on a platform, refer to the COSC (Combined Operation Safety Case) and inform the platform control room, if necessary.
•	Hold a Toolbox meeting with all relevant personnel to discuss the operational and safety issues.
•	Take measures to prevent dynamic shock loading when landing off conductor on BOP deck, if applicable.
•	Ensure scaffolding is ready and conductor cutter in place in plenty of time, if necessary
•	Ensure that sufficient labor and handling gear is in position on the required deck as soon as possible.
•	Ensure that while drilling the top hole (36" & 26") to use the manual elevators, if possible.
•	Use correct length bails when picking up the conductor.
•	Ensure that the ROV is in position well before it is needed.
•	In a sub-sea application, ensure that the conductor and BHA are painted white.
•	Ensure that the spacer and mix water is ready for the cement job.
•	Ensure that the displacement figures are ready and double-checked.
•	If Merlin connectors, ensure that the clamp, power unit and back-ups are serviceable.
•	Ensure to inspect the conductor, inside and out, to ensure there is no scale, obstructions, tack welding, etc.
•	Inspect the float joint to ensure clean and clear, also check float.
•	Check to ensure that there are no welds/seams are in slip / elevator areas.
•	Verify that the elevator fits on the conductor before start of the operation.

2.8.2	DRIVING CONDUCTOR
•	Ensure that exact conductor depth is known.  If depth is not given, then drive to refusal.  A rule of thumb for would be 300- 350 blows/foot.
•	On a Jack-up, to reduce the time of the operations and when it is practicable, drivable threaded quick connectors (i.e. the RL-4) and drivable squnch joint connectors such as the Fast Realizing Joint (i.e. the ALT-2) should be used.
•	Ensure proper drive shoe
•	Ensure you have a contingency plan if pipe does not drive to requested depth (clean out or drill and drive).
•	Ensure proper equipment on location
1.	30" conductor pipes as per the Drilling Program (squnch joints, rapid lock connectors or welded preparation).
2.	Pile hammer.
3.	Equipment for handling joints.
4.	A 31" false rotary table, to ensure better pipe stabbing, shall be positioned on top of the rotary table
5.	ROV or divers to ensure proper threading through guides on platform.
6.	Welding machine, if using welded connections.
7.	26" bits.
8.	If quick joint is to be used, the following equipment shall be available:
•	Hydraulic tong 30” type Joy AA -X.
•	Two hydraulic clamp 30” 250t.
•	Side door elevator.
•	Hydraulic power unit.
•	Ensure conductor is secured to structure.
•	Ensure that proper cutoff depth below rotary table is known.

2.8.3	DRILLING AND CEMENTING
•	Ensure proper equipment on location
1.	26" bit + float 
2.	36" Hole Opener 
3.	1 x 9" Monel DC 
4.	1 x 9" Spiral DC 
5.	HWDP 
6.	Drill Pipe
7.	30” Stab-in float shoe and auxiliary equipment
8.	Pad eyes to anchor conductor to rig structure
9.	Sufficient cement to cement 30”
10.	Mud materials enough to drill hole, plus materials for mixing kill mud.
•	Ensure to tag bottom with the bit on initial run and note and record the following on the IADC and daily report:
1.	RKB to water level
2.	RKB to sea floor
3.	Water depth
4.	Time of day (for tide allowance)
•	Ensure first two joints are drilled with 80- 120-funnel viscosity mud.  Finish with fresh water or seawater.
•	Ensure pumps are run at half speed for first two joints or until hole opener is buried.
•	Ensure a maximum rotary speed of 50 RPM should be used to spud the well and until the bit and hole opener are buried, then gradually increase the rotary not to exceed 100 RPM.
•	Ensure high viscosity sweeps are pumped every connection.
•	Ensure hole is circulated clean at TD.
•	Ensure to displace hole with gel mud and short trip at TD. (If jack up, do not pull bit above sea bed)
•	Ensure conductor is cemented with inter string.
•	If the 30-inch connectors use snap type rings, Ensure enough "0" rings and locking plugs are on board.  Also, if the casing is pulled for any reason, a connector-releasing tool will be required.
•	Ensure once the casing shoe is below the water level; fill the casing with sea water to the sea level as each joint is run (approximately 30 bbl/joint).

2.8.4	JETTING IN 30 INCH CASING
•	Ensure conductor is prepared
1.	Tally conductor joints
2.	Clean and inspect connectors as per manufacturers guidelines
•	Replace all damaged or questionable box O-rings
•	Apply a light coat of multi-purpose grease and re-install thread protector
3.	Prepare running tally
4.	Paint shoe jt white + 20 ft from bottom
5.	Paint 2” black rings at 5’ intervals and number 5-10-15-20
•	Ensure wellhead equipment is prepared
1.	Ensure that service personnel are on location two days prior to running equipment for pre-operational check of tools and equipment
2.	Install beacon and bulls eye on PGB
3.	Place mud mat and PGB on spider beams.  Check for any damage and set up per Installation Manual
4.	Check guidepost anchor pins are secured and ROV accessible.  Ensure guideposts are compatible to the BOP stack receptacles
5.	Paint black numbers on guideposts 1-2-3-4 corresponding with G.L.T’s.  Paint black rings at 1 ft intervals on posts.
6.	Tally the 30” housing + extension joint and the running tool
7.	Ensure anti-rotation tabs are on 30” housing
•	Ensure casing running equipment is prepared
1.	Check all casing handling equipment for cracks or defects. Decommission and/or replace any defective equipment.
2.	Inspect all slings, shackles, lifting eyes, etc. prior to picking up the first joint of casing
•	Ensure the drill string will have the bit spaced 6" outside the casing shoe. Have beveling band on location to cut 30" shoe if space out cannot be obtained from available pipe, subs, etc.
•	Make sure that all the bull plugs have been removed from the 30" wellhead running tool.
•	Record the minimum flow rate to operate the motor. (Will be required to jet the first joint.)
•	Ensure all pad-eyes are removed flush to casing.
•	If the 30-inch connectors use snap type rings, Ensure enough "0" rings and locking plugs are on board.  Also, if the casing is pulled for any reason, a connector-releasing tool will be required.
•	Ensure once the casing shoe is below the water level; fill the casing with sea water to the sea level as each joint is run (approximately 30 bbl/joint).
•	PGB structure should have two level indicators.  The levels should be checked to insure they are in good working condition and that they agree.
•	Ensure to paint a bold vertical white line on the stem of the running tool continuing onto the main body of the running tool
•	Ensure the conductor string is P/U with handling tools in place to ensure the running tool is locked.
•	Ensure the anti-rotation keys on the PGB are aligned with the slots on the housing.
•	Ensure a pick up test is performed to confirm that the PGB is attached to the conductor housing
•	Ensure all the components of jet string are drifted.
•	Ensure the jet string weight is recorded.
•	Ensure that the running tool is not over torqued.  The torque put in the tool at surface must be overcome subsea before the tool can be retrieved.
•	Remove safety plate from lock ring.  Failure to remove the plate prior to running subsea will prevent the PGB from being retrieved remotely
•	Verify the space-out of the jetting string inside the 30" using the ROV.
•	After lowering to mud line:
1.	Record string weight.
2.	Do not turn on the mud pumps because of the danger of twisting the guidelines by the reactive torque of the mud motor.
3.	Space out to spud so the last 90’ will be jetted with one full stand (No connections the last 90’).
•	Ensure run to approximately 5' below the mud line and a check of vertical and PGB alignment made using the ROV.
•	Ensure to record the following on the IADC and daily report:
1.	RKB to water level
2.	RKB to sea floor
3.	Water depth
4.	Time of day (for tide allowance)
•	Ensure pumps are started slowly and begin jetting/motoring process.
•	Ensure spoils are monitored in the 30" running tool return ports to adjust pump without aggressively jetting the 30".
•	Never slack off more than the buoyed assembly weight that is below the mud line.  This is to minimize the chance of building angle while jetting
•	Ensure that PGB does not turn and become twisted during the jetting.
•	Pump gel sweeps and work pipe as necessary.
•	When the 30" housing is 25' above the mud line, slow pump rates as required to stop the PGB 12' +/- 1' above the mud line.
•	Conductor markings will be monitored by the ROV throughout the final jetting operations to ensure that over jetting will not occur.
•	Ensure PGB alignment is checked with ROV.
•	Ensure that angle of PGB is checked.  If over 1.5, pull conductor.
•	Ensure the 30" string is held in place for 1 hour before releasing the running tool.
•	Ensure wellhead depth relative to RKB and wellhead inclination from both slope indicators are recorded on drilling report and on wellhead data drawing.
•	Ensure running tool is disengaged from the 30" housing prior to POOH.  Run a drift survey and strap out the jetting string.

2.9	DIRECTIONAL DRILLING OPERATION GUIDELINES

2.9.1	FUNDAMENTAL DIFFERENCES - DIRECTIONAL VS. CONVENTIONAL DRILLING
•	Mud viscosities may need to be higher, especially in tectonically stressed areas.
•	More horsepower is required for cleaning, motor and ancillary equipment losses
•	Optimized connection practices to minimize possibility of sticking pipe.
•	Optimized trips, increased possibility of stuck pipe, remove cutting beds, etc.
•	Special attention to "low side" cuttings removal.
•	Additional drilling trends monitoring requires a higher level of documentation.
•	Measured Depth to True Vertical Depth conversion requires special attention.
•	Directional drilling results in more formation exposure.
•	Reservoir differential pressures have a larger effect.
•	Fractured reservoirs and vuggy reservoirs impose a greater fluid loss risk.

2.9.2	CLASSIFICATION OF DIRECTIONAL DRILLING

2.9.2.A	SHORT RADIUS
Radius of curvatures between 45' - 125', with build rates of 30 degrees/100 Ft. The angle build assembly generally requires specialized equipment such as knuckle joints in the BHA, along with articulated stabilizers and can be accomplished with conventional surveying equipment. It does not require a steerable assembly. The horizontal section however, is usually limited to less than 1000'

2.9.2.B	MEDIUM RADIUS
Radius of curvatures between 286' - 700' with build angles from 8-30 Deg./100'. Medium radius allows for a greater horizontal extension. The angle build BHA is usually comprised of a bit, a stabilizer equipped with low speed, high torque motor (depending on compressive rock strength), an orienting bent sub, a non-mag collar, and MWD equipment, then proper weight. This assembly is not rotated, and is used for the "build" section.

2.9.2.C	LONG RADIUS
Radius of curvature rages from 1,500' - 4,500' plus, with builds of 2 Deg./100' - 6 Deg./100'. The curvature allows for conventional casing and assemblies, and steerable motors. Rotation is usually slow, and driven by a top drive, or power swivel, to facilitate continuous pipe movement and circulation.

2.9.3	SPECIFIC CONSIDERATIONS FOR DIRECTIONAL DRILLING

2.9.3.A	DRILLING FLUIDS
2.9.3.A.1	LESS THAN 45 DEGREES
•	High yield point and laminar flow, high viscosity and high density sweeps.
2.9.3.A.2	GREATER THAN 45 DEGREES
•	Low yield point and turbulent flow, low vis sweeps in turbulent flow.
•	Horizontal cleaning is best accomplished with scouring turbulence while laminar flow results in poor low side cleaning. Erosion and cleaning is sometimes difficult to balance. Therefore, mud lubricity and a low friction system becomes critical.

2.9.3.B	BIT SELECTION
•	Diamond bits and PDC's can also help the cleaning issues as they produce finer cuttings.

2.9.3.C	MUD TYPE
•	Consider bi-polymer systems.

2.9.3.D	HYDRAULIC CONSIDERATIONS
•	Torque and drag trends indicate the effectiveness of hole cleaning efficiency.

2.9.3.E	ACTIONS TO CONSIDER
•	Pipe rotation with movement to stir cuttings, hole sweeps with pipe rotations, short trips with rotation and high yield sweeps. Consider ECD effect at TVD. Friction can become a real factor in the ECD equation as the annulus extends horizontally.

2.9.3.F	WIPER TRIP GUIDELINES
•	When significant torque and drag are noted, or increasing.
•	When in a permeable zone for a lengthy time.
•	After rotating for (15) or so hours since the last short trip to the casing shoe
•	As other hole conditions dictate.
•	Maintain a special vigilance watch on slack off drag forces. Record slack off weight with pumps off for drag criteria to establish rig drag limits.
•	The top drive should be used to back-ream the first 5-10 stands out at maximum strokes. Slack off with the pumps off to set the slips. If the slack-off weight falls below the slack-off limit, repeat reaming until the desired slack-off weight is achieved. If drag increases as the stabilizers enter a gauge hole section, slack off, engage the top drive, establish circulation on the down stroke, ream out of the hole.

2.9.3.G	GENERAL GUIDELINES
•	Ensure a brief history of the well related to directional operations highlighting failures, outstanding achievements, recommended methods for the next well, proposed changes to standard procedures, improvements on previous well, etc.  This should be kept up to date during the drilling of the well and included in the End of Well Report.
•	Drill the straight section of the hole to kick-off point as vertical and straight as possible.
•	On the envelope of each single shot survey disc (if used), and also on the directional drilling reports, the following additional information is required (where applicable): 
1.	Magnetic instrument serial number and inclination range.
2.	Spacing of compass from bottom of NMDC assembly (ft).
3.	Total length of NMDC assembly used (ft).
4.	Length of normal steel assembly (ft) used below NMDCs.
5.	Spacing of compass from wireline rope socket (ft), and diameter of wireline used (inch).
•	Ensure survey readings and the calculated results shall be passed at least twice per day to the Project office.
•	Ensure that the WSS shall extrapolate the well course, particularly where wells are in close proximity, in order to predict possible collision points or danger areas.
•	Ensure the single shot gyro drift is checked immediately after recovery.
•	Ensure that the observed drift is reported on the single shot envelope.
•	Ensure that the gyro survey is rerun if the drift exceeds 5 degrees
•	Ensure in large diameter casings and in near vertical casings to properly centralize multishot instruments
•	Ensure that sufficient length of nonmagnetic drill collars (NMDCs) required to give sufficient protection against magnetic influences are run in the string.
•	Ensure that dogleg severity does not exceed the programmed build-up rate.  If dogleg severity exceeds 1/100' above BUR, contact Project office about taking corrective action.
•	Ensure that center jet and large nozzles are utilized in directional hole to reduce erosion.
•	Ensure that the length of the bottom hole assembly is kept to a minimum.
•	Ensure-hole accuracy checks of MWD equipment are made prior to commencement of each drilling run. Apart from the repeat check survey at every station while drilling, a minimum of two other check surveys per run are recommended.
•	To provide a check on the repeatability of survey data, two sets of readings shall be taken at every station while drilling ahead.
•	Ensure great care is taken to avoid rough handling of MWD equipment; crane drivers should be advised accordingly.
•	When opening the hole to a larger size, use a bull nose (a 1/2 inch under hole size) on a DP pup joint below the hole opener. Leave the NMDCs in the HO assembly so that check surveys can be taken, and ensure that no new hole is drilled.
•	Ream out excessive doglegs with a stiff HO assembly rather than with a stiff drilling assembly. If a new hole has to be started higher up, then use a stiff drilling assembly and watch out for twist off.
•	All hole sections drilled initially with a deflection tool (Dyna-Drill, turbine, etc) shall be reamed when running in with a follow up assembly, in order to smooth out doglegs.
•	Minimize bit weight in the build-up section to avoid excessive doglegs, and use the highest practical rpm to maintain the best hole course.
•	Make corrections to direction as soon as errors are observed.
•	If continuous excessive over pulls are observed when tripping, do not change the assembly to a stiffer one, rather leave out non-essential stabilizers and drill collars and use more HWDP.
•	Avoid rotating freely off bottom in deviated holes. This is because in marginal cases it could be possible to exceed the DP fatigue limit in a dogleg, with the full DC weight added to the tension of the pipe rotating through the dogleg.
•	If extremely high torque is encountered when drilling deviated holes, then consider the following (arranged in order of priority):
1.	When drilling with water-based mud, add a non-polluting torque reducer or lubricant to the mud.
Note:	Do not use diesel or base oil.
2.	On the next trip leave out some stabilizers and DCs (replace with HWDP).
3.	Analyze the mud for problem factors; improve the quality or increase the gradient.
4.	Replace the NB stabilizer with a roller reamer (if available).  Be aware that this may act as building assembly.
•	Place a magnet in the flow line from the start of drilling each well to collect metal cuttings and shavings coming out of the hole
•	Ensure instruments are returned to shop for checking, calibration and repair routinely after every well.
•	Ensure where there is wear on the DP due to metal to metal contact down hole (e.g. worn, polished or shiny patches on the pipe or tool joints) to carry out the following preventative (corrective) action: 
1.	Check alignment of derrick over the center of the well.
2.	Check the wear bushing.
3.	Use lowest practical rpm, and consider the use of turbines or mud motors.
4.	Use a bottom hole assembly of minimum practical weight.
5.	Do not rotate with the bit off bottom.
6.	Use and check the magnet in the flow line for metal cuttings.
•	Ensure that hard facing on tool joints is one of the smooth types or alloy such as Parkalloy.

2.9.3.H	SPECIFIC CONSIDERATIONS FOR DIRECTIONAL DRILLING
•	If the drill string becomes stuck (see also Stuck_Pipe_Guidelines) because of high drag or torque, or pulling into a tight spot, work the drill string down-hole by reciprocating and applying torque with pumps off, jarring down if possible (however, do not torque while jarring down). If the BHA is not freed, attempt to circulate while jarring down. Spot a release pill as necessary.
•	If the drill string becomes stuck due to differential sticking, utilize same procedure as above, except jar up and down, holding torque on down stroke.

2.9.4	DYNAMICS IN HORIZONTAL / HIGH ANGLE DRILLING

2.9.4.A	WEIGHT ON BIT (WOB)
•	The primary WOB component must be in the vertical, or near vertical section of the hole. HWDP is typically run from the end of the weight supplying collars in the vertical section, through the build section and into the horizontal section. HWDP is therefore in compression and must be used to avoid buckling. However, conventional drill pipe may be placed in compression in long horizontal sections, within buckling stress limitations. A guideline is to use heavy components in well sections with less than 60-70 degrees inclination and lighter components at higher inclinations.

2.9.4.B	BITS
•	The change from a slide mode to rotating creates a larger hole. The greater the degree of bend, the larger the hole. Eccentrically induced side loading can also come into play. This increase stress on the lower cone, or lower gauge of a fixed cutter bit, accelerating wear. This can also result in high torque and damage the motor stators. Therefore, it is important to optimize the steerable assembly and bit, maintaining a common axis as much as possible. If a bit fails to maintain angle, the reverse of this problem occurs, leading to extra correction runs.
•	At high angle, the string absorbs more weight and borehole interface, resulting in inadequate WOB, hence lower ROP.
•	Abrasive drill cuttings build up on the low side will attack bits in a similar manner, making hole cleaning even more imperative.
•	Conclusions
1.	Strengthened gauge rows and gauge sections.
2.	When possible, drill the tangent and horizontal section with the rotary and without a steerable assembly. The trade-off is a reduction in directional control and increased BHA changes.
3.	Ensure the "bend" angle is as near the bit as possible.
4.	Aggressive fixed cutter bits, hardened across the face and reinforced up the gauge will withstand high RPM.
5.	A low RPM motor can improve bit life, the trade off is ROP. Also, a less aggressive fixed cutter or gauge re-enforced tri-cone will also improve on-bottom life, but again the trade-off is ROP.
6.	Trajectory problems complicate bit selection. Steerable assemblies require bits that are capable of swift corrections. Bits with long gauge protection are difficult to steer, while one with short gauge protection better facilitate steering. PDC pads can also enhance steerablity.
