7.	Maintaining an angle in a tangent section present another problem. Longer gauge and protection are required to resist drilling angle change.
8.	All of the "trade-offs" must be taken into account, but as a rule, the less time the hole can be drilled in while maintaining excellent cleaning capabilities, results in a better wellbore. Consider a reasonable trade-off of depending on the build section, then tangent, then laying down a steerable assembly for the horizontal. Target limitations will help in this decision.  Certainly, upgrading bit gauge requirements in the horizontal section should be considered, if a steerable system is used.
9.	Azimuth control trajectory represents still yet another problem. Generally, roller cones walk to the right, fixed cutters walk to the left. Consider walking tendencies in the overall path trajectory. There are two solutions: Consider higher RPM, less weight with a steerable system or a more aggressive bit to take advantage of weight available at the bit.
In Summary: the bit selection for the type of formation is probably the best choice. Improve the gauge or roller cone leg pads. Fixed cutter bits will steer more readily and extended gauges will hold more effectively. Steerable assemblies increase control, reduce BHA changes, but require added attention to bit selection and gauge protection.
Other points to consider:
1.	Roller Cone bits for difficult steering situations.
2.	Tortuosity means that actual curve rates will be 2 deg/100ft above planned.
3.	Motor sliding ROP is usually one half of rotary ROP.
4.	Directional tendency is the result of BHA, bit and formation tendency effects.
5.	Formation tendency means that the wellbore will tend to drift in the direction perpendicular to the plane of dip.

2.9.5	REFERENCE
Wellbore Surveying and Collision Avoidance

2.10	DIVERTER OPERATIONS CHECKLIST

2.10.1	GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS
•	Refer to the Drilling Program for the requirements of the diverter operations.
•	Confirm that all required equipment for its rig-up is on board / on location and serviceable.
•	Identify any heavy lifts and notify OIM / Rig Superintendent of requirement and timing.
•	Ensure to hold a Toolbox meeting with all relevant personnel to discuss the rig-up, operation and safety issues.
•	Diverter systems should comply with API RP 64 (Diverter Systems Equipment and Operations).
•	Ensure the diverter bag is large enough to accommodate the running of casing.
•	Ensure that the diverter has 2 outlets positioned 180 apart that are large enough diameter, i.e. - 12" or larger for offshore rigs, and 10” or larger for land rigs, and is long enough to divert any fluids away from the platform/rig.
•	Ascertain that all lines are straight and unrestricted. All bends to be targeted and  90 bends are to be avoided.
•	Diverter lines are to be securely anchored, especially at bends and at end of lines.
•	Verify suitable response time: All functions should be completed within 30 seconds for annular elements up to 20" dia.  For elements greater than 20", the closure should be within 45 seconds.
•	Ensure that the valves are full opening.  Ball valves are preferred.  Butterfly valves are not to be utilized.
•	If diverting is required, open diverter discharge line first, then close shale shaker valve prior to closing diverter bag.
•	Automatic sequenced operation is always preferable to manual operation.
•	Diverter systems should be fully function tested and flushed after installation or at regular intervals.
•	Install kill line through diverter system to inject water in system to reduce the risk of explosion or fire.
•	Diverter control system should be capable of operating diverter from two locations.  One should be at the driller's console.
•	Consider a premix mud for safety; refer to the Drilling Program and/or company policies. See: Well Control Manual.
•	Evacuate all non-essential personnel if diverter operations are initiated.
•	Consider the added ECD effect while diverting
Note:  Diverter Operations are not usually successful and the main goal is to save personnel from harm.  This operation buys time to get people off the rig!

2.11	DRILLING AHEAD GUIDELINES

2.11.1	GOOD DRILLING PRACTICES TO CONSIDER
•	Monitor drilling trend changes, i.e. ROP, torque, drag, pressure, stroke changes, background gas, connection gas.  If connections are repeatedly 'tight' consider making a check trip
•	Continuously move the pipe.
•	Monitor connection pump engagement pressures.
•	Maintain vigilance for fill-up on connections.
•	Conduct pit measurement and vigilance,
•	Know down hole and surface volumes.
•	Monitor returns continually.
•	Monitor pump pressure and MWD data looking for washouts, downhole/surface torque and weight anomalies or faulty down hole motors.
•	Monitor water volumes for mud mixing. An increase in water requirements indicates a solids build-up. Refer to the Drilling Fluids Checklist.
•	Pay attention to ditch magnets, wear indicators, and wear bushings.
•	When RIH, wash the last couple of stands to bottom.  When near bottom, slowly rotate while gently feeling for the bottom of the hole.  When bottom is "found", drill with very light loads and low rotary speeds for about 10 min then slowly increase weight and rotary to planned drilling parameters.
•	Vary the drilling parameters to determine the optimum parameters, as required, i.e. new bit, different formation, etc. (Drill Off Test)
•	Monitor all drilling parameters when reaming.
•	Avoid creating excessive dog legs.  In soft formations they are a potential source for key seats and dog legs at shallow depth cause high drag and torque values for the remaining drilling operations.
•	Keep open hole covered at all times.
•	Avoid the Critical Rotary Speeds for vibrations and harmonics.
•	Avoid high RPM in keyseat or doglegs.
•	Avoid rig maintenance during planned operations and tripping.
•	Use controlled drilling rates to avoid overloading of the mud in the annulus with cuttings.

2.11.2	PROPER HOLE CLEANING CONSIDERATIONS
•	Pumping sweep pills (hi, low vis).  On high angle holes, if viscous pills do not appear to improve hole cleaning, use low vis pills.
•	Sufficient flow rate for hole (annulus) size.  Rule of thumb is 50 –60 gpm/in hole size.
•	While drilling, monitor cutting returns at the shakers to assess hole cleaning efficiency.
•	Keep sand and silt content of the mud as low as possible.
•	Verify any torque and drag change after sweeping the hole.  If improved, consider maintenance sweeps.
•	Variable rates for circulating, circulating time before connections to ensure adequate time.
•	Be aware of hole correct volume and lag time.
•	Circulate hole until returns are clean prior to tripping out of hole or cementing casing.

2.11.3	HYDRAULICS CONSIDERATIONS
•	Optimized hydraulics is the key to good drilling performance.
•	For bit hydraulics, refer to Bit Checklist.
•	Consider utilizing center jet bits in 12 ¼” holes and larger.
•	Ensure system losses are minimized to maximize nozzle velocity and HSI, if required.
•	Insure pumps are equipped with properly sized liners for anticipated flow rates and pressures.
•	Determine the optimum cleaning rate for a given hole section, that is the maximize rate necessary to drill and clean the hole (Carrying Cuttings Index, minimizing "balling" tendencies) while maintaining laminar flow for a given rheological properties.
•	Ensure that extraordinary pressure losses been taken into account, i.e., losses through motor or directional equipment.
•	Verify if there any special drilling program requirements affecting hydraulics
•	Ensure that mud pump diagnostics have been performed to determine efficiencies and verification of flow rates.
•	If pump pressure is increasing to kick open floats, this is an indication of increasing ECD's, cutting, poor hole cleaning, consider increasing circulating time or sweeping the hole.  If initial pump pressure is increasing, this will be an indication of the ECD weight.
•	Ensure that proper size liners are installed in the pumps to adequately deliver optimum circulating volumes and pumps pressure.
•	If optimum pressure and flow rates for drilling cannot be maintained due to pump problems, pull into casing and repair pumps.  Do not continue drilling at reduced rates.
•	Ensure that a water meter is used to monitor water additions to mud system.

2.12	DRILLING FLUIDS CHECKLIST

2.12.1	PREPARATION TO DRILL
•	Ensure that the mud inventory is correct at the loadout point and that dry goods are per the drilling fluid program. 
•	A detailed mud program shall be included as an integral part of the drilling program.
•	No variation from the mud program is permitted without previous discussion with and approval of the Project Drilling Engineer.
•	The mud characteristics to be used for specific operations, such as tripping, casing running, etc., shall be based on specifications described in the relevant sections of the Drilling Program.
•	Ensure that the drilling fluid program on board and is appropriate for unanticipated events and formations.  Refer to: Barriers
•	Ensure that the mud engineer has reviewed the relevant topics in the drilling program and has a detailed mud program for each hole section.
•	Verify that the proposed chemicals by the mud company are needed and suitable.
•	Review any possible tool and bit plugging before introducing loss materials. Ensure that all the loss materials are compatible with down hole tools, motors, MWD equipment, if not, identify any alternatives. Refer to: Loss Circulation Guidelines.
•	Ensure that the supply boat has the minimum amounts of barite and base fluid on board, or other weighting materials on board at all times. Consider additional inventory for anticipated well conditions.  Refer to: Minimum Chemical Stock
•	Verify with the Mud Engineer that the mud lab is adequate and all equipment is in working order.
•	Review mud testing requirements with mud engineer onboard
•	Review anticipated drilling operations and problems with mud engineer and rig crew at Toolbox meetings or any other appropriate time.
•	Review daily reporting requirements with mud engineer and appropriate rig personnel.
•	Review systems monitoring equipment and emergency shutdown systems with rig crew, mudloggers, and mud engineer.
•	Ensure that the Drilling Contractor has real time reporting systems, in addition to electronic monitors for pit vigilance, gains, losses, etc. This could include something as simple as a rope gauge installed in the pits.
•	Review piping and valves, pumps, all mud related equipment and tanks with all relevant personnel, to include system vigilance.
•	Review expectations of mudlogger: sampling criteria and descriptions as required by geologists, monitoring criteria, special functions such as "D" Exponent vigilance.
•	Test all base fluids for contaminants, always test mix water salinity.
•	Verify hydraulics for mud and loss materials being utilized, especially flow rates, carrying capacity indices and systems pressure losses.
•	Verify that appropriate safety equipment is positioned strategically on rig. Review company safety procedures with all personnel. Eye-wash stations should be positioned nearby tanks, mixing and cleaning equipment and personal protective gear such as rubber gloves, rubber aprons, goggles, and dust masks should be available and used
•	Determine system capacities and hole volumes.
•	Review utilization of trip tanks.
•	Check that there is a monitoring system: monitoring of returns, flow rates, gas detection equipment, hazardous fluids equipment.
•	Agree on rig communications requirements for mud transfers and other events such as trap and tank cleaning.
•	Develop best operational practices to reduce potential risks.
•	Review fluids handling and mixing criteria including appropriate safety requirements.

2.12.2	RECEIVING / HANDLING OF DRILLING FLUID PRODUCTS
•	It is recommended that an independent surveyor verify before and after delivery to the boat all volumes of drilling fluid products.
•	Ensure that a sample of the drilling fluid drawn from the loading tanks is sent to the rig prior to loading for verification of the properties.
•	Ensure that the barite is checked by the Mud Engineer for contamination prior to use.
•	Ensure that the sack store has adequate handling equipment for the anticipated mixing.
•	Ensure that all liquid emulsifiers are delivered to the rig site in bulk containers to reduce handling risk of individual drums by rig personnel.
•	Prior to loading on the supply boat, make certain all pallets are properly protected. Pallets should be shrink wrapped and banded.  Consider jungle wrap for offshore and extreme conditions.
•	Confirm several types of loss circulation material is loaded out; graded calcium carbonate, fibrous materials, flake materials, etc, or as per mud program.
•	Review mud sampling and weighting criteria with rig personnel.
•	Review expected mud properties and proper maintenance with mud engineer and appropriate rig personnel and mud loggers.
•	Ensure that mud properties, rheology promotes maximum cleaning and ROP performance.
•	Meter the water and brine additions to the drilling fluid. Report usage daily.
•	Slug the drill pipe with mud from the standby reserve.
•	If returns are lost, immediately fill the annulus with water. Measure the volume of water required and continue filling the hole until the hole stands full. Do not pull the drill pipe out of the hole until the hole stands full.
•	If offshore, the standby boat should be topped up with barite at all times. Never transport barite in tanks that previously held cement, except in an emergency.
•	Ensure that all MSDS and other safety data sheets are on board for all materials.

2.12.3	DRILLING WITH OIL-BASED MUD

2.12.3.A	ISSUES TO CONSIDER WHEN DRILLING WITH OIL BASE MUD
o	To avoid pollution, precautions shall be in place (drains from the rig floor and shale shakers, cuttings treatment, etc.) in order to avoid environmental spillage.
o	The safety of the crew shall always be paramount to prevent oil mud/skin contact and accidents such as tripping, falling, hand crushing, etc.
o	Maintain the cleanliness of the rig.
o	Oil resistant equipment (BOP rubbers, various pack-off, seals on mud pits, etc.) must be used.  
o	Control of air quality in the pits and shale shakers room is a main concern when oil base mud is used and the capacity of the ventilation system should be at least one complete air change every minute.
o	For fire control, the ventilation system shall keep the air/gas mixture below the combustible limit, however in case of fire, the system shall be automatically shut down as the blowers would feed the fire with fresh air
o	Well control is affected by the use of oil-based mud as it can create hazards while handling drilling gas and gas kicks. Since a gas influx may dissolve completely into the drilling fluid, small influxes of gas are more difficult to detect. Gas expansion and pit gain do not occur as the influx is circulated toward the surface. Detection may be delayed until the influx is only a few hundred feet from the surface when the well suddenly starts to flow. Usually there is little time for the rig crew to react to divert the flow. If this occurs, large volumes of mud and gas may be unloaded onto the rig floor and up into the derrick.
o	Since there is no equipment or equipment arrangement to take care of the undetected gas influx as described, the problem of gas influx has to be overcome by careful planning.
o	The Drilling Contractor and the Company shall prepare and agree on a set of specific rules and procedures.

2.12.3.B	BASIC GUIDELINES
o	When drilling or coring known gas formations, be aware of potential for gas break out and sudden unloading.
o	When back on bottom after tripping with gas formations exposed to the open hole, be alert to sudden unloading of the hole as bottoms up near surface.
o	A suspected but not detected influx shall be circulated to a predetermined distance below the BOP stack (e.g., 500ft), the annular or upper pipe rams will then be closed and bottoms up circulated out through the choke, under control to the mud/gas separator.
o	Ensure the rig floor is sealed with combing in the working area around the rotary table. A drip pan should be located under the rotary table and drain into either the flow line or sand traps. Drainage from the pipe rack area should be designed such that mud spills can be diverted to either the drip pan or the rig drainage (offshore) during heavy rains. A 3" to 6" combing should be welded to the deck separating working and nonworking areas to contain spills.
o	Ensure a steam cleaner or high-pressure low-volume cleaner is used for housekeeping rather than conventional water hoses. This minimizes contamination of the oil mud with wash water. All water lines should either be bull plugged or locked, and only one locked water line should be designated for any water additions to the mud.
o	An outside pipe wiper should be placed below the rotary bushing to reduce spillage during trips. Inside wipers reduce spillage in the racking area.
o	The use of a mud saver valve is advised. Besides reducing the amount of oil mud spilled, it can also be a timesaving device.
o	Offshore, the rat hole and mouse hole must be sealed. This can be accomplished by welding a reducer to the end of the pipe and installing a valve. Any oil mud that accumulates in either pipe can then be drained into a drum and pumped back to the mud system or disposed of properly.
o	Since the sand traps cannot be dumped when using oil mud, mud guns should be installed in them so the mud and solids can be stirred and agitated for further processing. The solids can be processed through normal solids removal equipment.
o	Agitators are very important for oil muds. Barite settling can be a problem. This is also true in storage tanks.
o	All possible spill areas should be cordoned off with combing. This includes the mud pump area, centrifugal pumps, and mud hoppers.
o	All tanks and open areas should be covered or closed off to prevent water intrusion.  Also, good ventilation must be maintained. Normally 90 air exchanges per hour is sufficient.
o	The mud laboratory must be well ventilated and explosion proof. Increased testing requirements may require additional electrical outlets.
o	Due to the solubility and violent breakout of gas in oil mud, particular attention must be paid to kick-monitoring equipment. PVT must be able to detect a 5-bbl increase
o	Oil muds require mechanical shear to form the emulsion. Venturi-style hoppers are the minimum required. A high-pressure shearing device is preferred.
o	Hoppers should be placed above the pits, if possible. This prevents oil mud from U-tubing and spilling over the hopper if the jet nozzle plugs or the feed pump stops.

2.12.4	MUD ENGINEER
•	Help to develop a team spirit with the rig personnel to certainly include the Mud Engineer as a focal point with the rig crew.
•	Ensure mud engineer attends Daily Rig Site Morning Meeting.
•	Ensure the same Mud Engineers are assigned to rig for the entire drilling of the well.
•	Ensure that the mud engineer has or develops a skeptical attitude, in not accepting hearsay and not making assumptions.
•	Ensure that he maintains a good dialogue with all the rig personnel.
•	Ensure the Mud Engineer, as a matter of daily routing, discusses all mud requirements / treatments with the Drilling Supervisor.
•	Ensure that all instructions are to be written and copies of all the instructions maintained in an organized file system.
•	Ensure that all mix requirements for the Derrickman is written and copies maintained in an organized file system.
•	Ensure that the Mud Engineer enforces the use of PPE for all the personnel while in the pits and around the shakers.
•	Ensure the Mud Engineer conducts a risk assessment of all mud transfers with the relevant personnel
•	Develop pit discipline with all relevant personnel.
•	The Wellsite Supervisor shall be notified immediately of any change in mud properties.
•	The Wellsite Supervisor and Driller shall be notified immediately of any variation in mud weight, chloride content, gas or any other property that may indicate significant changes in the formation drilled and/or entry into overpressurised zones.
•	A full mud check shall be carried out three times during a 24 hour interval whilst drilling. Two of these checks are to be made before the drilling reports at 06.00hrs and 14.00hrs and the third around 18.00-20.00hrs.
•	Mud weight and funnel viscosity shall be recorded at least every 30mins at the flow line and suction pit.
•	Properties of reserve and kill mud should be checked and maintained daily and recorded the mud report.
•	When circulating gas cut mud and/or bottoms up, the following data shall be recorded:
1.	Mud weight
2.	Salinity
3.	Maximum gas
4.	Pit level
5.	Interested volume
6.	Depth and time.
•	The Mud Engineer shall check mud weight at the shakers and downstream of the degasser continuously when circulating gas cut mud and/or bottoms up.
•	Rheology shall be checked three times a day or more frequently if requested by Wellsite Supervisor.
•	Project Manager shall previously approve any addition of oil to the water base mud system.
•	A thorough inventory of mud stock will be made on a weekly basis.

2.12.5	MUD TESTING EQUIPMENT
•	Verify with the Mud Engineer that the standard test equipment is on board:
1.	At least two mud cups and funnels.
2.	Six speed viscometer including thermal cup and hot plate.
3.	Pressurized mud balance.
4.	Standard retort.
5.	Sand content test kit.
6.	Titration for calcium chloride in oil mud.
7.	pH meter.
8.	Emulsion stability meter (digital).
9.	Electro-hygrometer.
10.	High temperature - high pressure filter press capable of testing to 350°F.
11.	The new API approved replacement solvent (Propylene Glycol Normal Propyl Ether (PNP)).
12.	Magnetic stirrer.
13.	Weight scales accurate to 0.01 gram and up to 2.5 kg minimum.
14.	Oven, with roller mechanism, capable of temperature ranges in excess of the anticipated bottom hole temperature and appropriate aging cells (Stainless Steel is preferred) for use in thermal aging tests.
15.	The capability of evaluating static shear gelation with Static Shear Test Tube.
16.	Kit to check both mud and formation cation exchange capacity.
17.	Garret Gas Train for testing acid gases.
18.	At least one (1) backup spare for each major piece of equipment such as VG, Retort, filter press (API & HPHT) etc. should be held in reserve. 

2.12.6	MUD TESTING
•	Ensure a daily mud management check list is available.
•	Ensure that a Fann 70 rheometer is available onshore to measure the rheology under down hole pressure and temperature of samples sent in.
•	Ensure that barite sag is measured daily.

2.12.7	SOLIDS CONTROL
•	Solids control is the key to performance drilling!  Excess solids:
1.	Reduces ROP	
2.	Increases PV, YP Gels
3.	Inhibits proper mud cake development, essential for hole stability
4.	Increases circulating pressure due to "sloppy" mud cake.
5.	Increases loss circulation and differential sticking problems.
6.	Decrease bit life.
7.	Increases chemical inhibition problems (reduces effectiveness of inhibition)
8.	Promotes shale swelling and sloughing.
9.	Increases maintenance and reliability for in line equipment (Pumps, MWD etc)
•	The primary function of solids removal equipment is to minimize the volume of mud required to drill an interval by concentrating drilled solids in their discard.  
•	Verify shaker capacity with anticipated volume of fluids to be planned in a given hole section.
•	Ensure that all equipment is sized for maximum anticipated circulating rates. This should include centrifugal pumps feeding cleaning equipment.
•	Verify shaker screen sizes appropriate for a given operations and anticipated drill cuttings removal. This could include loss materials as appropriate (Refer to: the Loss Circulation Guidelines.
•	Depending on the type of downstream cleaning equipment available, ensure that a process is in place to control cleaning operations. Coordinate the policy, cleaning procedures with the rig crew and mud engineer.
•	Ensure proper operation of mixing equipment, tanks jets, paddle mixers, tank turbolizers and any other maintaining and cleaning aids.
•	If cleaning system bypassing is required (pill circulating, etc.), especially shakers, make certain that over-cleaning is monitored and avoided.
•	Ascertain that the largest solids removal equipment follows the shakers: desander, desilter, and centrifuges, in that order.  Every piece of solids removal equipment should take suction from an upstream compartment and discharge to a compartment downstream

2.12.8	SHAKER OPERATING GUIDELINES
•	Screen mesh size should be fine enough for mud coverage of 60% to 80% of the surface area.  If mud is being dumped due to excess volume or high drilled solids content, slight mud losses over the screen can be tolerated.  Mud lost in this manner will have a higher drilled solids content than the rest of the mud in the active system
•	The shaker should be level and tack-welded to the pits or its own support structure.
•	The proper voltage and frequency for the drive motor must be provided.  Low line voltages reduce electrical system life.  
•	The vibration must be rotating in the proper direction.  The top of the shaft should rotate towards the solids discharge end.  
•	Install new screen squarely.  Ensure there are no bad wrinkles in screen.
•	Special care must be taken to install proper and clean-screen support cushions.  Screen support cushions should be changed each time screens are changed.
•	Special care must be taken to tension screens properly in accordance with manufacturer's recommendations.  Improperly tensioned screens last a fraction of their expected life.  Should re-tighten once every 24 hrs. for first three days of operation.
•	A water hose should be available in the shaker area so the screens can be washed and inspected during trips.  Use base oil for oil or synthetic based fluids.
•	A water spray is occasionally used on shaker screens to remove gummy particles (gumbo) from the screen.  It should not be used routinely.  Water from the spray dilutes the mud and drives smaller particles through the screen that would have otherwise been removed.  Note: never use water with OBM.
•	Never bypass the shaker even during a trip.  Bypassing the shaker allows slugs of large solids past the shakers and into the system.  These will plug the equipment downstream

2.12.9	FEED PUMP GUIDELINES
•	Each bank of hydroclones should have its own feed pump but cyclones are of little value if the rig has good linear shakers.  Only use should be with WBM for reducing mud weight by removal of solids.
•	Do not use Mud Cleaners. They are counter productive as they generate fine drill solids
•	Mud entering the suction compartment should come only from upstream equipment and equalizing backflow from the downstream compartment.
•	The pump impeller and motor must be sized according to the manufacturer's specification.
•	The proper size feed and suction lines must be selected.  Oversized lines cause solids settling in horizontal sections and undersize lines cause excessive erosion.  
•	The use of elbows, tees, and valves should be minimized.  One valve in the suction (for pump repair) and one in the discharge (to reduce the load on the electric motor at start-up) is sufficient.
•	Anything that deforms or reduces the hydrocyclone's apex opening should be removed.  By reducing the fluid volume lost, it reduces the amount fine solids removed and causes excessive wear in the apex area of the cones.  

2.12.10	CENTRIFUGE OPERATING GUIDELINES
•	Ensure operating instructions are available and personnel have been instructed in correct operation.  
•	Do not operate the centrifuge without belt guards in place.
•	Before starting the machine, manually rotate the bowl to ensure free movement.	
•	Do not operate the centrifuge if unusual noise or vibrations develop.  Lubricate the bearings as per supplier recommendations (every 8 hours of operation is typically recommended).  
•	Allow the unit to obtain full operating rpm before slurry feeding is begun.
•	Do not overload the bowl with solids.  Indications that the bowl is being overloaded include the following:  
1.	Safety torque coupling frequently disengages
2.	Unit packs off rapidly
3.	Excessive amounts of weight material in the overflow
•	Use lower feed rates and higher dilution volumes with heavily weighted and viscous fluids.
•	Ensure proper agitation is provided in the centrifuge suction pit and the solids return tank.
•	Turn off the dilution liquid after shut down.
•	If a problem develops that is not understood, call a technician before attempting experimental solutions.
•	Some brands of centrifuges use shear pins to protect the gearbox from overloading.  If the shear pin breaks, replace it with another authorized pin and correct the overload conditions.  Do not put in a makeshift pin because it could damage a gearbox, which is the most expensive part of the machine.
2.13	DRILLING STRING CHECKLIST
•	Always know how many joints of drill pipe are on the drill site.
•	Always be familiar with the drilling string on board. Check all crossovers, subs, and handling tools available for operations and verify tool joint compatibility. Know all drill string weights, grades, and tool joint types. Inspect and fully caliper, OD and ID all rig provided crossovers and subs. Request and verify component tests (DS5), x-ray, blacklight inspections and other appropriate tests.
•	A drill string design analysis should be done and updated at regular intervals.
•	Develop a list of, and order any additional components that could be required for operations, including basic fishing tools for all drill string components and service provider BHA components
•	Visually inspect all drill string components and verify test documentation.
•	Depending on the drilling conditions an average time to inspect the drill string is after approximately 300 rotating hours for the BHA, and 50,000 ft. of hole for the drill pipe. A full DS-5 inspection should be expected if receiving an "unknown history" string of pipe from a drilling contractor. Any tools to be used in the drilling well from a contractor must have a Certificate of inspection showing it passed the inspection, before being used in the well.  If well is of critical nature (i.e. deep HTHP), a DS-1 inspection should be performed.
•	Make certain that items such as Kelly saver subs, surface safety vales have appropriate thread and/or crossovers.
•	Verify maximum stresses and pressures that the drilling string can be subjected, post and keep all personnel aware of drill string limitations. This includes torsion and bending moment limitations (section modulus).
•	Never run drill pipe in compression.  Keep in tension by providing 10- 15% excess BHA weight.
•	Verify and check all tool joint makeup requirements and ensure compliance on the rig floor.
•	Utilize only accepted API makeup torque values.
•	Limit rotary torque to 80% of recommended tool joint make up.  Utilize torque-limiting devices. 
•	Periodically check & calibrate rig torque gauges.
•	Ensure all tool joints are clean before makeup; apply only acceptable API pipe dope.
•	Encourage rig floor vigilance for identifying any problems in makeup or overall pipe integrity.
•	Be aware of all drilling string and components' cycle time.
•	Be aware of prior unexpected failures and consider full black light inspection if necessary.
•	If questions arise as to drilling string integrity, make certain they are addressed prior to operations. Refer to: Rig Move Checklist.
•	Review vibration criteria and causes, use of shock subs, critical rotating speeds
•	Ensure knowledge of dynamic factors such as buoyancy, drilling string pressure losses, hook load.
•	Be aware of tension and stretch information for stuck pipe considerations. See Stuck Pipe Guidelines.
•	Remember to rotate drill pipe on trips.  Alternate breaks each trip.
•	Always be aware of capacities of the entire string and BHA.  Ensure displacements are always known and complied with!
•	Remember to regularly inspect and change the kelly saver sub as it mates with every joint of drill pipe in the string.
•	Drill string vibrations and severe "whipping" of the pipe in the rotary table may be due to induced harmonics. However, harmonic vibrations in a drill string are very damaging if action is not taken to eliminate these vibrations. There are Critical Rotary Speeds (CRS), which will induce resonance in the drill string.  Under ideal conditions, these can be accurately calculated and/or measured in situ.  The well bore, however, presents far from ideal conditions and calculations will probably not give you THE critical rotary speed.  At best they may give you an idea of about where you may expect to see harmonics and from this you can estimate a rotary speed range to avoid.
•	Tool joints should be hard faced with a smooth or alloy type compound.  Avoid coarse and sharp hard facing.
•	Consider drill pipe rubbers inside of cased hole, especially if high rotating speeds are anticipated. Rubbers should be approximately 1/4" OD greater that tool joints and placed one joint apart. Inspect on each trip. Do not run drill pipe rubbers in open hole
•	Slips should be set about 3' above rotary to avoid bending moments.
•	Ensure an inspection Policy is developed utilizing the following criteria
1.	Inspection program to be used
2.	Acceptance/Rejection criteria
3.	Ensuring inspections are done properly
4.	Inspection frequency
Note:  The T.H. Hill Associates, Inc. Standard DS-1, Drill Stem Design and Inspection is a guide to setting up an appropriate inspection program and should be used to assist in setting up your program.

2.14	DRILL STEM TESTING (CASED HOLE) CHECKLIST

2.14.1	DST CHECKLIST
•	Ensure that the testing program is reviewed and that all relevant personnel understand all the objectives of the test.
•	Ensure that the Pre-DST checklist is filled out.
•	Ensure that Hazop/Hazid has been performed.
•	Ensure all safety equipment meets local regulatory standards.
•	As part of the design process for the testing string, calculations should be performed by the DST contractor and confirmed to determine the likely maximum contraction and expansion of the string during the various phases and operations of the test, i.e. circulation, production, injection (acid or water injection test), killing, etc.
•	Calculate capacity of hole from perforations to bottom of hole.
•	Calculate capacity of anchor assembly from CIP sampler to perforations.
•	Calculate volume of influx require before formation fluid has reached sampler.
•	Calculate time required to get sample in CIP sampler based on flow rate.
•	Ensure clock range is proper for test being conducted- not to long.
•	If open hole test, ensure proper selection of packer seat by log and drilling parameter analysis.
Refer to: 	Pre-DST Checklist

2.14.2	EXPECTED FLUID
•	Verify sampling procedures with the service personnel and requirements (reservoir PVT, assay work) as per the program, considering
1.	Surface vs. bottomhole samples
2.	Pressurized vs. atmospheric sampling
•	Check maximum possible operating pressures (gas column vs. oil column) during the test.
•	Verify potential flow rates.
•	Verify whether H2S and/or other poisonous and/or corrosive fluids can be expected.

2.14.3	BOTTOM HOLE ASSEMBLY, PACKER, TUBING, WELLHEAD,  BOP AND TEST TREE, COFLEX, SEPARATORS, TANKS AND CHOKE MANIFULD
•	Check pressure and temperature ratings vs. requirements
•	Visually inspect all tools to ensure no damage was sustained in transit particularly to threads and sealing surfaces.
•	Ensure all required crossovers have been sent and physically checked for correct threads. Measure crossovers and note length, ODs and IDs. Particular attention should be paid to the IDs of rented crossovers.
•	Ensure all tools are dressed with correct shear pins and rupture discs.
•	Account for string expansion and annular pressure increases as temperature increases during flow.
•	Verify packer rubbers are in good condition.
•	Fully understand functionality of all tools
•	Carefully check BHA tool ID specifications vs. requirements.  Drift all equipment to ensure full ID for wire line, CT, and Surface Readout tools to be run in hole.  Under most circumstances the minimum ID should be 2.25”.
•	Ensure packer will withstand differential pressure encountered.
•	Have backup BHA on location (if appropriate).
•	Witness pressure gauge programming.
•	Ensure a reversing valve- pump out sub is run.
•	Ensure a TIW valve is run one joint below test head and chick-san to test head is long enough to allow pick for operation of TIW.
•	Witness strap from top shot to RA tag.
•	Review pressure-testing procedures.
•	Obtain a BHA schematic with tool straps.
•	High pressure tests or tests of gas zones should be performed through 2 7/8-inch or 3 l/2-inch tubing; preferably with a modified API coupling or appropriate premium connection.
•	Test strings should preferably be internally plastic coated.

2.14.4	SURFACE TEST EQUIPMENT
•	Confirm deck space and variable load limitations, if flow-testing separators, tanks, air compressors are to be brought on board.
•	Ensure cellar is jetted prior to test if applicable.
•	Ensure all high-pressure lines are secure and tested.
•	Verify any heavy lifts comply with relevant safety practices.
•	Check methanol, de-foamer, de-emulsifier and other chemical requirements.
•	Ensure there are seawater pumps available for adequate boundary cooling
•	Burner booms - review positioning, type (i.e. "clean burners"), prevailing wind, drip pans, workboat and barge location.
•	Ensure an adequate supply of nozzles; monitors, sprayers, etc are on board.
•	Verify that there is adequate air compressors for the burners to ensure a "clean" burn, with zero, or minimal, drop out.
•	Ensure that the equipment is inspected and ready prior to being sent to location
•	Ensure that equipment is adequately protected during shipment.
•	Understand functionality of all surface equipment.
•	Review pressure-testing procedures.
•	Ensure Surface Testing equipment is pressure tested on the rig before starting a well test; 
1.	For all pressure tests, the area outside accommodation must be clear of non-essential personnel.
2.	Pressure tests shall be carried out using water. Each pressure test shall be recorded on a record sheet and the pressure shall be held for a minimum of 15min.
3.	Test pressures shall be specified on testing program. However, devices protected by rupture discs should not be tested to more than 90% of working pressure.
4.	BOPs, choke manifold, choke and kill lines shall be pressure tested as per Well Control Policy.
5.	The following equipment of the surface package shall be pressure tested:
o	To end of burners.
o	To gas and oil diverter manifolds.
o	Through test separator to outlet valves and bypass valves.
o	To inlet valves and bypass valves on test separator.
o	To outlet and bypass valve on heater.
o	High-pressure side of the heater up to blank choke and bypass valve.
o	To inlet valves and bypass valves on heater.
o	Two upstream valves on production choke manifold.
o	Two downstream valves on production choke manifold.
The test shall be repeated whenever a connection on a line is broken out.
•	Obtain a schematic with all surface equipment and flow lines

2.14.5	EMERGENCY SHUT DOWN SYSTEM AND PROCEDURES
•	Consider surface vs. bottomhole shut-in.
•	Establish procedure for use of ESD (when to use it and under what conditions).
•	Consider location of ESD stations to place in conspicuous places around the rig.
•	Consider type ESD system, e.g. positive pressure system.
•	Ensure to function test the ESD actuator on the flow wing valve. The ESD is a fail-safe valve.
•	Review evacuation plan.
•	Obtain ESD diagram.

2.14.6	OPERATIONAL ISSUES
•	The well will be perforated only if there is a 24-hour forecast of good weather.
•	Two tested barriers in place or available to be closed at all times.
•	Permanently installed surface lines should be used with the minimum of temporary connections supplied from the surface-testing contractor. Ideally these temporary connections should be made-to-measure pipe sections with welded connections, however chick-sans can be used but must be tied down to the deck or earth.
•	Production testing can proceed at night. However, perforating should be delayed to permit the well to be opened at daylight.  First formation fluids to surface should also be in daylight hours.
•	The production test will be suspended on a forecast of 50-knot winds within eight hours. 
•	The production test will be suspended, the well shut in and the test string displaced to the SSTT whenever the vessel heave exceeds six feet.
•	Verify all requirements of HAZID, HAZOP and SIMOPS are met prior to the start of the test.
•	Ensure non-essential electrical systems are shutdown and all electrical equipment, junction boxes etc are approved for their respective operating areas.
•	Ensure pipe tally is reviewed and correlated.
•	Ensure to hold a Toolbox meeting with all concerned to discuss programme, all contingencies and relevant safety issues.
•	Ensure that derrick man has escape route and it is functioning properly.
•	Ensure that pump is rigged up to the annulus prior to starting test.
•	Ensure cementer remains in the vicinity of the cementing unit to pump into the well if required.
•	Specify data collection and reporting requirements.
•	Ensure fishing tools for all equipment to be used during testing shall be on rig.
•	Ensure adequate platform shall be available to operate the valves on the flow head.
•	Complete BOP test shall be carried out before starting well testing operations.
•	A tubing inspector or the Test Supervisor must be on the rig floor witnessing the make-up of all the joints that make-up the test string.
•	Ensure mud logger assists with taking samples and checks for H2S
•	Ensure cranes or other lifting equipment will not be operated over the production test equipment while the test is underway.
•	Ensure all non-essential personnel should remain in the living quarters and off the rig floor and catwalk when perforating and testing operations are underway.
•	Check service company manpower requirements (no operation or tools should rely on one person) & only essential personnel should be on the rig during the testing operation.  Ensure adequate number of test and safety personnel where they receive sufficient rest.
•	Review perforating procedures.
•	Check under-balanced perforating requirements.
•	Double check operating pressures, primary and back-up firing method.
•	Consider mud-conditioning issues prior to RIH with BHA.
•	Review correlating procedures.
•	Review the sequence of flows and shut-ins.
•	Review well kill procedures.
•	Ensure adequate volumes of mud and mud products available.
•	Ensure protection from heat damage is supplied where required, i.e. navigation lights, lifeboats, etc.
•	Check dump valves operations, separators and associated equipment, and ensure delivery to proper storage
•	Ensure that choke manifolds, bypasses, and flow lines are tested to specifications.
•	 Ensure that steps are taken to avoid "hydrates", ascertain that glycol and/or methanol is readily available.
•	 Ensure rig air compressor can't ingest raw gas from the test equipment.
•	Ensure that space out is correct on floating rigs and SSTT is not run in the blind/shear rams.  A dummy run shall be made for space out purposes.
•	Ensure that the annulus level is monitored through out the well test.
•	Ensure that the flare is monitored through final shut in.
•	Ensure that all formation fluids are reversed out or bullheaded back into formation prior to pulling test string.
•	Ensure that the DCIP valve is in reversing position prior to unseating packer as weight is required before it is rotated.
•	Ensure that pump is lined up on annulus prior to reverse out as mud will fall in annulus.
•	Ensure annulus and drill balance prior to concluding reverse out.
•	Ensure that close attention is paid to swabbing when pulling out after the test.  Any upward movement of mud in annulus after pipe stops should be treated as an emergency.  Check that hole is taking require volume of mud.  Use the trip tank and ensure mud is proper weight.
•	Ensure that position of down hole tools are known at all times.
•	Ensure TIW valve below test head is closed prior to unplugging or braking off test head.
•	If offshore, do not send all the samples taken during a single test on the same boat; split samples into complete sets and dispatch on different vessels.

2.15	ECD MANAGEMENT GUIDELINES

LISTEN TO THE HOLE - IT IS ALWAYS TALKING!

2.15.1	ECD GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS
•	These guidelines are provided as an aid in utilizing Equivalent Circulating Density (ECD) to mitigate fluid losses and are not intended to be considered direct instructions or contradict direct wellsite management instructions concerning drilling techniques, related to loss of fluids, placement of loss reduction material or interfere with well control issues.
•	ECD is primarily affected by hydraulics and mud weight.
•	Fluid losses can be more complicated when drilling in slim hole, fractured formations, or partially depleted intervals.
•	Ensure that all relevant drilling personnel have a proper understanding of ECD, to assist in identifying well control situations and maximizing drilling time.

2.15.2	UTILIZATION OF ECD FOR MIGRATION OF FLUID LOSSES
•	Calculate the ECD each tour and update when entering critical intervals of known or suspected loss potential, i.e. faulted/fractured, high permeability, or abnormally low pore pressure.
•	Review the drilling program for anticipated pore pressures and areas of anticipated loss circulation.
•	If through experience losses are anticipated, reduce mud weight by the calculated ECD.  Strong consideration must also be given to utilizing PWD and MI's Virtual hydraulics program.
•	Further mud weight reductions, with proper authority, may be made cautiously, subject to required hydrostatic pressure required for the section.
•	If the annulus is slightly underbalanced, when pump is shut down for a connection, the annulus will flow, therefore consider the following:
1.	Increase mud density by ECD margin prior to making the connection.
2.	Making a connection by stripping through the annular with pressure on the annulus.  THIS IS ONLY RECOMMENDED IF PLANNED AND APPROVED.
3.	Refer to: Contingency Stripping Procedure
•	If slight gains or losses are noticed while drilling, the pump rate can be utilized for control (notwithstanding an actual well control situation):
1.	Speed up pump for slight gain, or
2.	Slow pump for slight losses.  Ensure enough flow to move cuttings.
•	Before tripping, make certain that the required drilling mud weight plus trip margin is increased to accommodate the lack of ECD, if necessary.
•	Communicate these guidelines to all relevant personnel and maintain a diligent attitude concerning well control.

2.16	FISHING GUIDELINES

2.16.1	GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS
•	Always be familiar with the drilling string on board. Check all crossovers, subs and handling tools available.
•	Ensure BHA's have sufficient ID's to run string shot or chemical cutters.
•	Verify tool joint compatibility and know all drill string weights, grades and tool joint types.
•	Ensure all fishing tools have been inspected and serviced prior to beginning drilling operations.
•	Verify that the Drilling Contractor has all required basic fishing tools for the complete drill string and BHA being utilized, e.g.
1.	Overshots: short catch and long catch with appropriate grapples and guide shoes.
2.	Spears
3.	Basic milling tools and burning shoes.
•	Supplement contractors fishing tools to be able to catch non-contractor supplied BHA components.  All items in BHA should be fishable with external catch tools.
•	Depending on remoteness of location, consider having auxiliary fishing tools such as jet baskets, ITCO junk basket, cone busters etc on location.
•	Verify that all tools and BHA's to be run in the hole can be fished by checking the net ID's, OD's & lengths for in-hole fishing tool tolerances and fishing necks
•	Review the drilling program to anticipate any additional basic fishing tools that could be required for specific operations
•	Review the Stuck Pipe Avoidance Guidelines
•	Review the various "back-off" procedures to ensure familiarity, including a blind back off.
•	Ensure wireline-logging company furnishes a complete set of wireline strip over fishing tools while logging.
•	Consider maintaining string shot equipment aboard the rig in remote locations.
•	In cased hole, don't mill to long in one place.
•	Never rotate the fish out of the hole.
•	Time is of the essence in fishing operations.  If working in areas where differential sticking is prevalent, keep a tank of premixed spotting fluid on location.
•	Measure and fully caliper all recovered pipe out-of-the hole
•	Fully tally, caliper and measure all fishing tool assemblies. Verify that complete schematics with all dimensions are available.
•	Identify a competent Fisherman to be available, as required, prior to start of the drilling operations.

2.16.2	GENERAL FISHING AVOIDANCE GUIDELINES
•	Refer to the BHA Checklist & Drilling Ahead Checklist.	
•	Ensure all rig personnel are fully aware of all sticking hazards and early identification techniques.
•	Ensure all tool joints are clean before makeup, apply only accept API pipe dope.
•	Keep mud properties in good condition at all times.
•	Keep pipe moving.
•	Avoid slow connections.
•	Encourage rig floor vigilance for identifying any problems in makeup of overall pipe integrity.
•	Be aware of all drilling string and components' and fishing tools cycle time.
•	If questions arise as to drilling string integrity, make certain they are addressed prior to operations.
•	Be aware of tension and stretch information for stuck pipe considerations. Refer to Stuck Pipe Avoidance Guidelines.
•	Ensure that conventional drill pipe is not operated in compression.
•	Remember to rotate drill pipe on trips.
•	Always be aware of capacities of the entire string and BHA. Ensure displacements are always known and complied with!
•	Remember to regularly inspect and change the kelly saver sub as it mates with every joint of drill pipe in the string.
•	Verify critical rotary speed.
•	Consider drill pipe rubbers inside of cased hole, especially if high rotating speeds are anticipated. Rubbers should be approximately 1/4" OD greater than tool joints, and placed one joint apart. Inspect on each trip. Do not run drill pipe rubbers in open hole.
•	Verify drilling jars settings and optimum drill string placement.
•	Slips should be set about 3' above rotary to minimize bending moments.

2.16.3	OPERATIONS
•	Confirm the exact top of fish and what is consists of i.e., OD, ID & length of each piece.  Draw a diagram with all dimensions
•	If pipe is stuck calculate approximate stuck point utilizing pipe stretch.
•	When a twist off occurs and penetration rates are high, circulate the hole clean and spot a viscous pill before pulling out, but keep the string moving up and down to avoid causing a washout above the fish. Also circulate as required before latching on to the fish and avoid tagging the top of the fish.
•	If an overshot is run it is good practice to always run an extension.  This is especially important after the fish has been milled over, so that the grapple can catch on to unmilled pipe.
•	In a washed out hole attempts should be made with either a bent single or a wall hook.
•	Know the limitations off derrick, substructure (include pipe in derrick), and all hoisting equipment.
•	Prior to jarring always mark the string at the level of the rotary table. Tidal difference should be considered when jarring for long periods on a floating rig.
•	Every 6 hours inspect equipment during fishing operations.  Check for loose and unscrewed nuts and bolts.  Ensure that the gap is properly set on the Martin Decker.
•	Before jarring, clear floor of all unnecessary personnel.
•	If utilizing spotting fluids, caution should be taken to prevent reducing hydrostatic and allowing an influx of well bore fluid or gas.
•	Always run pack off in over shot.
•	Circulate over fish with pump at idle, watch for pressure increase as an indication of latching on.
•	Attempt to circulate through fish prior to jarring.
•	Jar with Kelly in string.
•	When it is not possible to jar with Kelly, elevator latch should be secured with rope or chain.
•	First attempt at a back off should be done one full joint above stuck point.  If unsuccessful attempt back off one stand above stuck point.
•	Remember that a successful fishing job considers the economics of fishing.  Confirm with the Drilling Office the number of days that can be spent fishing before it is cheaper to re-drill the section.  Keep this in mind while planning and attempting to recover the fish.
•	Know the limitations of the drill pipe and tools on each job.
•	Ensure to plan the fishing operation with the relevant rig personnel and hold a Toolbox meeting prior to rigging up to fish to discuss the plan and safety.
•	Review the drilling program to anticipate any additional basic fishing tools that could be required for specific operations
•	When running oil jars, consider adding a jar accelerator to increase the jarring action on the fish.
•	Taper taps are made of very brittle material and break easily. They should only be run as a last resort.
•	Spiral grapple type overshots and spears will handle heavier loads than the box or basket types. However, the spiral grapples are easier to damage and break while getting onto the fish, due to the brittleness of the spiral grapples. A mill guide should be run below the grapples if possible in order to dress off the top of the fish before engagement.
•	If string shot equipment is not available, consider manual back off only as a last resort.  Drill collars are seldom if ever backed off this way.
•	Confirm & record the following: weight of mud in hole, formation type, all casing and tubing sizes and weights, depths and deviations at liner tops, casing shoe, TD, top of fish etc, any dog legs and the severity.
•	If circulating sub is run in fishing string, strip down sub and install new shear pin prior to use (ESPECIALLY DURING ROUND TRIP AFTER JARRING).
•	If stuck, confirm if circulation is available, and if so, is it full or partial.
•	If stuck while pulling out of hole, attempt to work pipe down while applying torque (know limitations of pipe).
•	Confirm if the jars still functioning.
•	Try to determine the mode of sticking, i.e. differential or mechanical and the cause i.e. Keyseats, inadequate hole cleaning etc.  Refer to: Stuck Pipe Avoidance Checklist.
•	Determine the free rotational torque prior to sticking.

2.16.4	REFERENCE
Fishing Economics Calculator

2.17	FLUID DISPLACEMENT CHECKLIST

2.17.1	SURFACE PREPARATION
•	Confirm and understand the requirements for displacing well fluid in advance of required operation, by referring to the drilling/completion program or with additional instructions from the office.
•	Verify hole-cleaning requirements. ECD management prior to displacing.
•	Consider fluid loss preventative program prior to displacing.
•	Verify volumes of fluid returns, and that volumes of displacement fluid are adequate for the given hole size.	
•	Verify rheological properties and weights of displacement fluid and rheological properties and weights of return fluids to be expected
•	Verify and identify any heavy lifts to prepare the equipment and to notify the OIM.
•	Ensure a Pre Job Safety Meeting is held prior to bringing on any brines/drilling fluids.
•	Ensure that all proper PPE (personnel protective equipment) is available for the type of fluid being handled.
•	Ensure that all MSDS sheets are held on board with copies with the rig medic.
•	Ensure that high-density brines are treated with Brine Shield.
•	The density of hot brine should be corrected for thermal expansion.
•	Verify any hazardous operations, such as cleaning out OBM tanks, for identification of any additional safety procedures and/or equipment
•	Verify equipment, material and labor requirements in consultation with the mud engineer, Toolpusher, barge master and office engineering staff.
•	Ensure all equipment, material and additional labor required is ordered and available in advance of operation to minimize any critical operations time spent preparing and rigging up equipment.
•	Ensure that a well thought-out pit management/boat utilization plan is prepared to minimize any critical operations time for the following activities:
1.	Cleaning of tanks aboard the rig to accept new fluid (OBM, brine, etc.)
2.	Allocation of sufficient tanks to accept displaced fluid
3.	Butterfly valves should not be used not in a brine plant, as they are not always leak proof.
4.	Preparation of any spacers, need smaller tanks to mix and store
5.	Transfer of fluid to rig from the workboats
6.	Flexible hoses to transfer fluids should be brine resistant, e.g. be made of viton or neoprene rubber.
•	In the event insufficient tank space to separate new fluid from displaced fluid, confirm requirements for additional portable tanks
•	Ensure that all relevant rig personnel are invited to submit input for plan with the objective to prepare for the displacement off the critical path.
•	Verify if any additional labor is required in the preparation of the operation.
•	Summarize the various tasks in an Excel or Microsoft Project Gantt chart.
•	Verify any requirements for filtration or special well site treatment of new fluid.
•	Check fluid(s) compatibility.  May need to run compatibility tests between fluid in hole and displacement fluid (maybe even with the spacer), maybe mix ratios of 25/75, 50/50 and 75/25??
•	Salinity compatibility, ensure one fluid will not be adversely affected by another due to salinity of a particular fluid.

2.17.2	DISPLACEMENT OPERATIONS
•	Hold a Toolbox meeting with all relevant personnel prior to start of displacement operation.
•	Identify requirements for BHA to clean/scrape the casing prior to the displacement.
•	Ensure the availability of all required down hole tools, such as scrapers, bushes, circulation subs, X/O's, etc
•	Verify the required circulation rate for the displacement, and ensure that pumping equipment is up to the job. Throughout the drilling fluid conditioning process, the flow rate used should be the maximum practical rate. At a very minimum, the circulation rate should be just within the turbulent flow regime.
•	Ensure that a pumping schedule is prepared to displace in a controlled fashion, I.e. holding back pressure with the choke in the event that a heavy fluid is displaced with a lighter fluid.  Monitor bleed off of pressure to verify that casing is holding.
•	Verify that displacement operation can be carried out without having to make a special trip to clean out casing, i.e. displacement could occur immediately after drilling the shoe track and testing the casing, prior to drilling out into next section.
•	Ensure that samples are taken of any spacers used.
•	Check to see if it is feasible to displace the cement plug with the desired completion fluid.

2.18	FORMATION INTEGRITY TEST / FORMATION LEAK OFF TEST CHECKLIST
•	Verify Formation Integrity Test (FIT) or Formation Leak Off Test (FLOT) requirements with drilling program or instructions from the drilling office.
•	Perform ToolBox meeting, discuss any special procedures, precautions required, operational highlights, possible unexpected events and review previous FIT/FLOT experience for Lessons Learned.
Leak off Test or Shoe Test
•	Ensure that sufficient new hole is drilled, refer to drilling program.  Usually  10 - 15 feet.
•	Ensure that bit is in casing when leak off test is performed.
•	Ensure that the cement equipment is operational and has calibrated gauge tanks to pump and measure as small as 1/4 bpm increments but not more than ½ bpm.
•	Ensure that a shut in valve is installed between pump and pressure gauge.  Use this valve for test shut in rather than relying on pump to prevent back flow.
•	Ensure that a bleed valve is installed between shut in valve and pump.  Open this valve during shut in period.
•	Ensure that all surface equipment is tested and leak free.
•	Ensure that the pressure gauge to be utilized in measuring LOT pressure is appropriate. 
1.	Type:  Liquid filled 4” or larger.
2.	Range:  As low as possible.  To determine range multiply highest anticipated pressure by 1.25, then choose smallest range that exceeds this range.
3.	Increment:  The smaller the better.  For 2000 psi gauges or smaller, the dial increment should be 25 psi or less.  For pressure ranges above 2000 psi, increments of 50 psi ore less is recommended.
4.	Location:  Isolated from pump vibrations and pressure surges.  Don't mount gauge directly to a test line.  Connect gauge to the test line with a minimum 5 foot pressure hose.  Mount near displacement tank or pump stroke counter if possible.
•	Ensure that the mud is properly conditioned to have the same density throughout, measure the density accurately.
•	Ensure that on surface and intermediate casing, pressure test with the casing-casing annulus valve open and observe for possible channeling or communication.
•	Ensure that maximum allowable surface pressure is calculated.  Record maximum allowable pressure as a horizontal line on graph.
•	Plot predicted leakoff value as a horizontal line on graph.
•	Ensure that a casing pressure baseline is established and recorded on graph prior to drill out of casing shoe.
•	Ensure that table and graph is prepared prior to job to record and plot pumping pressure vs. bbl of mud pumped.
•	Ensure that test is pumped down drill pipe.
•	Ensure that pumping (LOT) is continued until pressure begins to level off.
•	Shut down pumps and record pressure in 1minute intervals until pressure remains constant or 10 minutes, whichever occurs first.  Record ISIP.
•	Ensure that if leakoff pressure test is much lower than anticipated, pressure is released and a second test performed.
•	Verify with the drilling office that the FIT/FLOT is valid, if not prepare for remedial action or to repeat the test.
•	Ensure copy of SPE/IADC 37589 Pressure Integrity Test Interpretation is on rig.

2.19	HPHT OPERATIONS CHECKLIST GUIDELINES

2.19.1	BACKGROUND
•	This document specifically covers the drilling of high-bottomhole-temperature and / or high-pressure sections of a well and details the possible limitations of the rig equipment.
•	There are no alterations to the normal chain of command for both emergency and normal operations.
•	The Offshore Installation Manager (OIM) is responsible for the welfare and health and safety of all persons on board and for the good practice and security of the installation.  He is the person on board the installation to whom ultimate authority for decision-making in respect of safety of the installation has been given.
•	There will be two Drilling Supervisors on the rig during drilling operations.  The Senior Drilling Supervisor is responsible for the technical direction of the well and ensuring that the well program is executed in a safe and efficient manner, communicating all requirements to the OIM, the Senior Toolpusher, and service company personnel. 
•	The Senior Tool pusher is responsible for supervising and directing the actual drilling activities.  The Driller has the prime responsibility for detecting an influx and closing in the well.
•	A meeting will be held at the start of each day to discuss the current and planned operations.  This meeting will be held by the OIM.  The Senior Supervisor, Night Supervisor, Senior Toolpusher, Night Toolpusher, Barge Engineer, and all relevant third party personnel should attend the meeting.  A written plan will be prepared after the meeting and sent to the Drilling Superintendent and the Rig Manager.  This plan will be distributed to all attendees of the meeting and it will be posted by the OIM in a conspicuous place.
•	The Senior Drilling Supervisor & OIM will report to their head offices at least once per day.
•	If there is any item of this document, which is unclear, the matter should be raised with your Supervisor before proceeding with operations.

2.19.2	HPHT WELL CONDITIONS
•	The drilling program instructions are to be followed in conjunction with these guidelines.  In any contradiction or omission, the drilling program takes precedence.
•	Identify and recognize well conditions:
1	Maximum anticipated bottom hole pressure (max BHP)
2	Maximum anticipated bottom hole temperature (max BHT)
3	Maximum anticipated shut in wellhead pressure (max SIWHP)
4	Hole section with HPHT conditions
5	Any other critical parameter, such as CO2, H2S, loss circulation
•	Ensure that the entire crew and service contractors recognize temperature and pressure limitations of all drilling equipment. 
•	Discuss the well conditions and verify the equipment limitations at a Toolbox meeting with the entire crew prior to drilling out into HPHT hole section.

2.19.3	SUSPENSION CRITERIA
•	The criteria for suspension of operations are as follows:
1	Severe lost circulation.
2	Failure of the BOP or wellhead to pressure test to maximum predicted surface pressure.
3	The operating pressure and temperature envelope of any component or material in the well, the pressure control system, or equipment and facilities connected to the well at surface is in danger of being exceeded and corrective action to prevent the danger cannot be taken immediately.
4	Insufficient quantities of mud, barite, cement, and glycol on board with minimum as follows:
o	Reserve mud: 400 bbls at the same weight as the active system.
o	Barite: sufficient to increase the active system mud density by 2.0 ppg and not less than 100 MT.
o	Cement: sufficient to mix slurry to fill the entire open hole at TD + 100% excess and not less than 75 MT
o	Glycol: 200 gals	
Note: These quantities are the minimum requirements for HTHP operations. For standard policy requirements refer to: Minimum Chemical Stocks
5	The cement unit, mud mixing facilities, or mud logging unit gas detection facilities become inoperable.
6	The temperature of mud returns at the surface indicates that the continuous temperature rating of the BOP is being exceeded or if the return mud temperature measurement system fails.	
7	The temperature measured at the drilling choke manifold while circulating out an influx indicates that the continuous temperature rating of the BOP is being exceeded or that the temperature downstream of the choke is < -20 degF or if the drilling choke temperature measurement system fails.
8	Excessive wear of casing, wellhead, or BOP components is indicated.

2.19.4	ABANDONMENT CRITERIA
•	Of the hole section is when wellbore integrity or primary well control cannot be maintained sufficiently to trip the drill string out of the hole safely after remedial measures have been attempted.  Examples of this type of situation may include severe lost circulation, repetitive losses/gains with high hydrocarbon levels, and severe differential sticking in a gas cap.

2.19.5	DRILLING OPERATION PRACTICES
2.19.5.A	GUIDELINES
•	The Drilling Supervisors, OIM, Toolpushers, Drillers, Assistant Drillers, Derrickmen, and relevant third-party personnel will attend meetings for discussion of the high-pressure well control procedures.
•	Ensure that all personnel are experienced and competent in HTHP operations.
•	A HAZOP should be conducted prior to the start of operations.
•	Keep the number of personnel on the installation to the minimum required to conduct the operations and maintain the installation.  
•	Conduct an accurate FIT after drilling the casing shoe, top of HPHT section.  If acceptable pressure integrity is not obtained, remedial action will be taken.
•	Make additions to the active mud system only after the drilling supervisor and toolpusher have given approval and the driller has been informed. Inform the mud loggers before and after any additions. Inform the driller and the mud loggers before any mud transfer/ processing pumps are switched on or off.
•	Clean, inspect, and test the drilling contractor's gas and H2S detectors and the mud logging contractor's gas and H2S detectors prior to drilling out the casing and weekly thereafter. 
•	Function test the mud/gas separator pressure and drilling choke temperature measurement systems at each BOP test.
•	Cross-calibrate all rig/mud logging/cement unit instrumentation at each BOP test. 
•	Equipment will not be operated out of their temperature and pressure limitations.
•	Inspect both auto chokes and the targets immediately downstream prior to drilling out the casing shoe. Inspection of the other chokes/targets will be carried out depending on the results of the autochoke inspection. Further inspections will be carried out during the drilling of the high-pressure section if deemed necessary.
•	Maintain a choke usage log.
•	Perform a rig acceptance inspection with an outside organization such as Moduspec or WestHou.  Heavy emphasis should be placed on the well control equipment.
Note:  Maximum anticipated wellhead pressure should be estimated based on a well that is evacuated of mud and results in formation fluid from TD to the surface.  Unless sufficient information is available, formation fluid shall be assumed to be gas.
•	Prior to drilling the casing shoe into the HPHT section, test the BOP stack; including the blind/shear rams to 300/BOP rating or maximum anticipated surface pressure.  Test the shear rams every second BOP test and after use.
•	Pressure test the casing prior to drilling out the casing shoe to test the liner lap. (Refer to the drilling program for details.)
•	Flush kill and choke lines from the top every 24 hours if displaced to mud to prevent plugging due to barite settlement. 
•	Each flow check will last for at least 15 minutes.  If any doubt exists, circulate bottoms up with additional flow checks at 50% and 75% of bottoms up. Rotate the drill string while checking flow.
•	Each time bottoms up is circulated or a flow check is made, pick up the drill string as required to position a safety valve in the work stand above the rotary table.
•	Prior to drilling the casing shoe, record flowback to trip tank after stopping circulation at full drilling rate
•	Have both crews perform a stripping drill prior to drilling out the casing shoe. On one of the drills, apply 500 psi to the well bore and strip in drill pipe.   Record the incremental pressure increase per stand stripped in on the Daily Drilling Report. In addition, record the minimum hydraulic pressure on the annular to affect a seal. Retain these values for reference if stripping is required.
•	Have both crews perform choke drills prior to drilling out the casing shoe.
•	Maintain a HPHT check list on the rig to keep a record of the periodic checks specified by this document.
•	Elastomers, rated for short duration operations at temperatures up to 350 deg F and as recommended by the manufacturer, shall be installed in BOPs, wellhead seals, and choke and kill valves. One set standard temperature variable rams will be installed.
•	Have a pack-off assembly ("shooting nipple") available on the rig for open hole logging.  Refer to: Barriers

2.19.5.B	PREPARATIONS
•	Ensure that the methods used by different crews or individuals on the rig for measuring and recording drilling parameters such as mud weight are consistent in order to obtain meaningful trends.
•	Keep at least 2 IBOPs for 5" drill pipe on the rig floor for stabbing.
•	Keep at least 4 full-opening 15,000 psi WP, H2S, drill string safety valves for 5" drill pipe, capable of sealing from above and below, on the rig.  Run 2 in the work stand, run 1 on the kill joint and keep 1 on the rig floor for stabbing.
•	Run a non-ported flapper-type float valve in all drilling assemblies.
•	Run a dart sub in all drilling assemblies.
•	Keep a wireline-retrievable drop-in dart in the driller's cabin.  Keep the retrieving tool on board.   Ensure that the dart passes through all drill string components above the dart sub, including the pump-in sub in the kill joint and all safety valves.
•	Run a circulating sub with shear-open ball seat when downhole tools susceptible to plugging by LCM are run, e.g., MWD, PDM, turbine, etc.
•	Utilize spiral drill collars with optimum stabilization.
•	Line up the BOP and choke manifold as per applicable instructions.
•	The mud/gas separator will be lined up as per applicable work instructions. Set values for the controller box alarm and the dump controller box signal to automatically vent will be calculated based on the mud weight circulated in the mud seal with the hot loop system as per work instructions. The driller will function test remote actuated valves from the choke manifold buffer chamber at the start of each tour and will pre-select the downwind line.  Log on IADC report.
•	Minimize the active pit volume to the extent that conditions permit in order to facilitate kick detection.
•	Check on the pit volume changes when the mud pumps, degasser, centrifuge or mix pumps start up or shut down. Record values and post in the driller's cabin and in the mudlogging unit.
•	Check the barite system every tower with air to confirm that transfer and high-rate mixing lines are clear.
•	Flush the trip tank and clean the float system every tower.  Keep 20 bbls minimum in the trip tank at all times.
•	Install ditch magnets in the mud return flow line.  Recover and weigh steel every tower.
•	Ensure that hard banding on drill pipe in cased hole is flush and smooth.
•	Ensure cement formulations for use across gas-bearing zones take into consideration the possibility of gas influx during cement operations.

2.19.5.C	DRILLING PROCEDURES
•	A work stand will be used for drilling.  Configure the work stand as follows (bottom to top): saver sub, safety valve, double, safety valve, single. The work stand will be used to permit access to a safety valve for shutting in the drill string during a well control incident, permitting the top drive to be removed and the kill joint and high-pressure hose to be installed in the event of high SIDPP. A work stand will be used for drilling.  Configure the work stand as follows (bottom to top): saver sub, safety valve, double, safety valve, single. 
•	A kill joint will be made up prior to drilling out the casing shoe.  Configure the kill joint as follows (bottom to top):  pump-in sub (4-1/2" IF connections with 2" Weco 1502 side outlet), safety valve, single.  2 Lo-Torq valves must be available on the rig floor for isolating the side outlet.  The kill joint must be easily accessible at all time and should be painted white.  The kill joint will be used for well kill operations when SIDPP > 2000 psi is measured or anticipated.  The kill hose will be connected to the side outlet via the 2 Lo-Torq valves.
•	Kick tolerance calculations must be kept up to date.  According to standards all kick tolerance shall be more than 25 Bbls. 
•	A 15-minute flow check will be made any time there is a drilling break while drilling.  Treat a drilling break flow check as a connection for the purposes of limiting the connection gas in the annulus.
•	Monitor the temperature of the mud returns while drilling. 
•	Mud weight in and out will be recorded by the pit watcher every 15 minutes and the driller will be informed.
•	Control rate of penetration as required keeping a maximum of one volume of connection gas (or gas from flow checking a drilling break) in the annulus at any time prior to penetrating the reservoir. After penetrating the reservoir sands, conditions may permit more than one connection gas in the annulus (e.g., pressure regression, confirmed static overbalance).
•	Consider increasing the mud weight if connection gas levels in the mud increase significantly.
•	Utilize PWD and LWD tools when drilling pressure section of hole.  Consider strongly the use of No Drilling Surprises technology.
•	If drilled gas levels in the mud increase significantly, consider closing the annular and circulating through an open choke to the MGS until gas levels return to previous levels.
•	Always attempt to establish rotation prior to circulation to break gels and avoid increased differential sticking tendency from ECD.
•	When the well is not being circulated with the mud pumps, line up on the trip tank and circulate across the hole with the hole fill pump, except during flow checks.
•	Ditch magnets should be used. The ditch magnets should be cleaned and inspected at regular intervals not exceeding 12 hours of rotation.
•	Keep the pipe moving to reduce the risk of differential sticking. Rotate and slowly reciprocate drill string.
•	Measure and describe wear on the wellhead wear bushing and report.

2.19.5.D	TRIPPING PROCEDURES - PRIOR TO TRIPPING
•	Inform the Senior Drilling Supervisor, the Toolpusher and the OIM prior to a trip.
•	Do not pull the drill string out of the hole if the well is flowing or continue to pull out of the hole if the hole fill volume is too low.  Do not pull the drill string out of the hole with severe losses.
•	The Drilling Supervisor and Toolpusher shall remain on the floor until the bit is inside the casing.
•	Calculate the maximum hoisting speed based on closed end swab/surge pressures (pumps off).
•	Line up trip tank with hole fill pump running.  Measure and record existing pit levels.  Driller and mudlogger to crosscheck pit/trip tank readings and fill in trip sheet. A trip sheet from the previous trip out of the hole should be available.  All pipe movements into and out of the hole will be monitored on the trip tank.
•	The Tool pusher is to provide the Driller with the necessary information about the trip, i.e., reason for trip, prevailing pore pressure regime, tripping overbalance, etc., and to ensure that the Driller and crew are fully aware of the correct well control procedures whilst tripping.

2.19.5.E	TRIPPING PROCEDURES - TRIPPING OUT
•	The following procedure applies to trips made prior to penetrating the reservoir or to the first trip.  After penetrating the reservoir, conditions may permit simplification of the procedure (e.g., pressure regression, confirmed static overbalance).  Differential sticking may pose a greater problem than well control once the reservoir is penetrated and its pressure is verified.  Keep the drill string moving as much as possible.
•	Flow check the well.
•	Circulate bottoms up to confirm static overbalance.
•	Flow check the well.
•	Break circulation and check pit volume.  POOH utilizing the trip tank and trip sheet.  If actual vs. predicted fills don't match at any time, tripping should be stopped and the drill string run back to bottom.
•	With the bit inside the casing shoe and circulating at the same rate, confirm that the decrease in pit volume corresponds to the drill string volume removed from the well.
•	Flow check the well.
•	Pick up the drill string at the maximum hoisting speed based on closed-end pipe swab pressure calculation.  Pick up one stand 5 times.  Check for swabbing.
•	Flow check the well.
•	Run in hole to TD
•	Circulate bottoms up.  If gas levels are expected to be high or are increasing, consider closing annular and circulating through an open choke as the mud from the open hole nears surface.
•	Flow check the well.
•	Break circulation and check pit volume.  POOH utilizing the trip tank and trip sheet.  If actual vs. predicted fills don't match at any time, tripping should be stopped and the drill string ran back to bottom.
•	With the bit inside the casing shoe and circulating at the same rate, confirm that the decrease in pit volume corresponds to the drill string volume removed.
